'((displaymath (m 3)) (displaymath (m 1/2)) (displaymath (m 3.14))) ...) produced exactly: <mfrac>31</mfrac>, <mfrac>12</mfrac>, <mfrac>7070651414971679.02251799813685248.0</mfrac> — the (number?)=>(mn) branch is dead for every real, and even the intended ratio case emits invalid MathML (mfrac needs two child elements, gets bare text). No test or caller in the repo exercises these handlers, and no comment documents this as intended.

#### [MAJOR] `aux.sxml.scm:211` — define-vhost route patterns ('/ p ...) can never match uri-path

define-vhost expands each route to (match/first (uri-path uri) (('/ p ...) b ...) ...). uri-common's uri-path returns a list whose first element is the bare symbol / — e.g. (/ "foo") — but in aux.base's match-pattern language a quoted symbol '/ i.e. (quote /) is NOT a literal pattern: it is destructured by the (x . y) pair rule as a two-element list pattern, which the symbol / can never match. So no clause ever matches and every request with a matching method raises 'match/first: uncaught value'. The literal-symbol pattern in this matcher is the BARE symbol, so the template must say ((/ p ...) b ...) instead of (('/ p ...) b ...).

**Evidence.** csi with (aux base): (match/first '(/ "foo") (('/ ,p) (list 'matched p)) (else 'nomatch)) => nomatch, while ((/ ,p) ...) => (matched-bare foo). End-to-end: a handler defined via define-vhost, invoked with current-request parameterized to a GET of /foo, raises 'match/first: uncaught value. (/ "foo")' instead of matching.

**Independent verification.** 1) Read /data/bitcoin/mnocentini-home/Developer/working-copies/aux.scm/src/aux.sxml.scm:202-212 — define-vhost expands routes to (match/first (uri-path uri) (('/ p ...) b ...) ...), and /data/bitcoin/mnocentini-home/Developer/working-copies/aux.scm/src/aux.base.scm:105-126 — match-pattern's syntax-rules literals are only (unquote unquote-splicing _); (quote /) hits the (x . y) pair rule and is destructured as a two-element list pattern, while the bare-symbol literal rule is the last clause. 2) Minimal repro with the specified csi: (match/first '(/ "foo") (('/ ,p) ...) (,_ 'nomatch)) => nomatch; ((/ ,p) ...) => (matched-bare foo). 3) End-to-end repro (scratchpad/vhost-repro.scm): (define-vhost (h req $) (GET (("foo") 'hit-foo) (() 'hit-root))) with current-request parameterized to (make-request uri: (uri-reference "http://example.org/foo") method: 'GET) prints: uri-path => (/ foo); handler => (RAISED match/first: uncaught value.\n\n(/ "foo")\n). 4) No test in src/test/ exercises define-vhost (only the import-library expander mentions it), so nothing masks or contradicts this; no comment suggests the quoted form is intentional.

#### [MINOR] `aux.sxml.scm:211` — define-vhost expansion references match/first which (aux sxml) never imports

The define-vhost template calls match/first, defined in (aux base), but (aux sxml)'s import list (lines 4-24) does not include (aux base). Because the identifier is unbound in the macro's definition environment, hygiene leaves it to resolve at the use site: a program that imports only (aux sxml) (plus spiffy/intarweb/uri-common) gets a handler that raises 'unbound variable: match/first' at request-dispatch time; it only limps along if the user happens to import (aux base) themselves. Fix: add (aux base) to (aux sxml)'s imports (which also makes the template reference resolve robustly at definition), together with the pattern fix above.

**Evidence.** csi script importing (aux sxml) spiffy intarweb uri-common spiffy-request-vars but not (aux base): invoking the define-vhost handler under a mocked GET request raises (RAISED "unbound variable" (match/first)). With (aux base) imported the same handler instead reaches the pattern bug above.

**Independent verification.** Read aux.sxml.scm: import list (lines 5-24) lacks (aux base); line 211's define-vhost template calls match/first, a macro defined only in aux.base.scm (line 83, define-syntax-rule). No test in src/test/ covers define-vhost. Ran two csi repros in the scratchpad against the compiled scratch repo. (1) vhost-nobase.scm — imports (aux sxml) spiffy spiffy-request-vars intarweb uri-common only, defines a vhost with a GET clause, invokes the handler under parameterized current-request with a mocked (make-request method: 'GET uri: (uri-reference "/hello")): output "(RAISED unbound variable (match/first))". (2) vhost-withbase.scm — identical but also imports (aux base): output "(RAISED match/first: uncaught value. (/ "hello") ())", i.e. the unbound-variable error is gone and execution proceeds into the separate pattern-mismatch bug. This matches the claim in every particular: hygiene leaves the unbound template identifier to resolve at the use site, so the handler raises at request-dispatch time unless the user happens to import (aux base).

### aux.kanren.micro.scm

#### [MAJOR] `aux.kanren.micro.scm:443` — absent° silently ignores compound (pair/structure) tags

Both μkanren-absento+ (line 437, pair clause at line 443, structure clause at line 446) and μkanren-verify-T+ (line 344, pair clause at line 350, structure clause at line 355) decompose a pair/structure term into its parts and only ever apply the tag predicate (which is (lambda (v) (not (equal? tag v))), built in absent° at line 541) to atomic leaves. A tag that is itself a pair or structure can therefore never be detected: the term is torn apart before it is ever compared, and every atom trivially differs from a compound tag. The test suite's own comment (test/absent°/datum in test/microkanren.scm: "any datum is a valid tag, as for absent?") says compound datums are intended to be valid tags, but only atomic tags are exercised there. Minimal fix for ground terms: in the pair and structure clauses of both functions, first test (μkanren-tag-pred? tag u*) on the whole walked subterm and fail when it matches, before decomposing. Full fix for partially-instantiated terms (e.g. q = (1 . x) with tag '(1 2) and x later bound to (2)) additionally requires keeping the absento constraint attached to non-ground pairs instead of eagerly decomposing, as faster-miniKanren does.

**Evidence.** Repro (CHICKEN 6.0.1pre1, compiled -O2 module): (°->list #f (fresh° (q) (absent° '(1 2) q) (=° q '(1 2)))) => ((λ () (cons 1 (cons 2 (quote ()))))) — an answer where q IS the tag; expected (). Same wrong success with the goals in the other order, and nested: (°->list #f (fresh° (q) (=° q '(0 (1 2) 3)) (absent° '(1 2) q))) also succeeds although (1 2) occurs inside q. Atomic tags behave correctly: (absent° 2 q) with q='(1 2) fails as expected.

**Independent verification.** Code reading confirms the mechanism: in /data/bitcoin/mnocentini-home/Developer/working-copies/aux.scm/src/aux.kanren.micro.scm, μkanren-absento+ (line 437) recurses into car/cdr on the pair clause (line 443) and into the slot list on the structure clause (line 446), applying μkanren-tag-pred? only in the atom clause (line 447); μkanren-verify-T+ (lines 344-356) does the same. The predicate built by absent° (line 541) is (lambda (v) (not (equal? tag v))), so a compound tag can never equal any atomic leaf and is never tested against a whole subterm. Runtime repro with the compiled module (CHICKEN 6.0.1pre1, /home/mnocentini/.local/bin/csi, scratch repo): (°->list #f (fresh° (q) (absent° '(1 2) q) (=° q '(1 2)))) => ((λ () (cons 1 (cons 2 (quote ()))))) — succeeds although q IS the tag; same wrong success with goals reversed, and for the nested case (=° q '(0 (1 2) 3)) with (absent° '(1 2) q). Atomic tags behave correctly: (=° q '(1 2)) with (absent° 2 q) => (), (absent° 5 q) => one answer. Intended semantics check: the test comment in src/test/microkanren.scm (test/absent°/datum, line 170) says "any datum is a valid tag, as for absent?", and the reference absent? in aux.base (src/aux.base.scm line 415, struct-absent? branch) does handle compound tags: (absent? '(1 2) '(1 2)) => #f and (absent? '(1 2) '(0 (1 2) 3)) => #f, while (absent? '(1 2) '(0 1 2 3)) => #t. So absent° silently disagrees with its documented reference semantics for compound tags. This is a bug, not intended behavior; only atomic tags are exercised in the tests, which is why it went unnoticed.

#### [MINOR] `aux.kanren.micro.scm:148` — Unification not reflexive for NaN: term containing +nan.0 never re-unifies with an equal term

μkanren-state-unify's number clause is ((and (number? u*) (number? v*) (eqv? u* v*)) (return)). In this CHICKEN build (eqv? +nan.0 +nan.0) is #f (CHICKEN compares flonums with =, and NaN ≠ NaN), so once a variable is bound to a value containing a NaN, unifying it again with an equal value fails: (and° (=° q +nan.0) (=° q +nan.0)) has zero answers, and so does (and° (=° q (list 1 +nan.0)) (=° q (list 1 +nan.0))). Only the eq? fast path saves the same-box case. Chez and Racket return #t for (eqv? +nan.0 +nan.0), so canonical miniKanren on those hosts succeeds here — this is a CHICKEN-semantics interaction, exactly the class this pass was asked to check. Fix: extend the number clause to also return when both sides are NaN, e.g. (or (eqv? u* v*) (and (nan? u*) (nan? v*))) with nan? from (scheme inexact). The same eqv? is reachable via disequality trial unifications, so ≠° involving distinct NaN boxes is affected symmetrically.

**Evidence.** csi repro: (print (eqv? +nan.0 +nan.0)) => #f; (μkanren-run (q 2 #f) (and° (=° q +nan.0) (=° q +nan.0))) => (); (μkanren-run (q 2 #f) (and° (=° q (list 1 +nan.0)) (=° q (list 1 +nan.0)))) => (); with the same flonum object n bound once, (and° (=° q n) (=° q n)) => 1 answer (eq? fast path), confirming the failure is exactly the eqv?-on-distinct-NaN-boxes path.

**Independent verification.** Read /data/bitcoin/mnocentini-home/Developer/working-copies/aux.scm/src/aux.kanren.micro.scm: μkanren-state-unify's number clause is exactly ((and (number? u*) (number? v*) (eqv? u* v*)) (return)), preceded only by the (eq? u* v*) fast path. Ran a repro script (scratchpad/nan-repro.scm) with the specified csi (/home/mnocentini/.local/bin/csi) and scratch repo. Output: "eqv? on nan: #f"; (μkanren-run (q 2 #f) (and° (=° q +nan.0) (=° q +nan.0))) => () ; (μkanren-run (q 2 #f) (and° (=° q (list 1 +nan.0)) (=° q (list 1 +nan.0)))) => () ; with the same flonum object n bound to +nan.0, (and° (=° q n) (=° q n)) => 1 answer (eq? fast path); with two guaranteed-distinct NaN boxes ((/ 0.0 0.0) twice, eq? => #f) => () ; control with 1.5 => 1 answer. Every element of the reviewer's evidence reproduced verbatim. grep for "nan" in the module and in src/test/*kanren* found nothing, so the behavior is neither tested nor documented as intended.

#### [MINOR] `aux.kanren.micro.scm:501` — fresh° bare-symbol form silently reinterprets first goal as var list

The destructuring clause `((fresh° α (β ...) body ...) (fresh° (α) (fresh° (β ...) (=° α (list β ...)) body ...)))` matches ANY use where the first operand is a symbol and the second is a list — which includes the classic miniKanren typo/idiom `(fresh° x (goal x) ...)`. The first goal is then silently consumed as a list of fresh-variable NAMES: `(fresh° x (null° x) (=° q x))` expands to `(fresh° (x) (fresh° (null° x) (=° x (list null° x)) (=° q x)))`, where inner bindings shadow both the relation `null°` and `x`, and `(=° x (list null° x))` fails the occurs-check (inner x occurs in its own binding), so the whole goal fails. The user gets zero answers with no error instead of the intended `(())`. syntax-rules cannot test identifier-ness, so the practical fix is to make the destructuring form syntactically distinct (e.g. a marker as in `(fresh° α ← (β ...) body ...)`, or a separate fresh°/list macro), so a goal in second position becomes a match failure (expansion error) instead of silent misbehavior.

**Evidence.** (import (aux kanren micro)) (μkanren-run (q 3 #f) (fresh° x (null° x) (=° q x))) => () — silently no answers; the intended (fresh° (x) (null° x) (=° q x)) gives ((λ () (quote ()))). No expansion error is raised.

**Independent verification.** Read src/aux.kanren.micro.scm lines 496-504: the clause at line 501 is verbatim as claimed. Ran with the custom csi and scratch repository: (μkanren-run (q 3 #f) (fresh° x (null° x) (=° q x))) => () with no error or warning, while the intended (fresh° (x) (null° x) (=° q x)) => ((λ () (quote ()))). This reproduces the silent zero-answer failure exactly. Also checked src/test/microkanren.scm (lines 79, 133, 141): the bare-symbol destructuring form is documented (comment at line 496) and intentionally used, confirming the feature exists and that its shape collides with the (fresh° x goal ...) typo pattern.

### aux.fds.sbral.scm

#### [MAJOR] `aux.fds.sbral.scm:38` — cdr/sbral else clause missing parentheses returns string instead of raising error

The else clause of cdr/sbral is `(else error "cdr/sbral: not a valid sbral")` — the call to `error` is not parenthesized, so the clause body is two expressions: the variable reference `error` followed by the string literal, and the string is returned as the clause's value. Calling cdr/sbral on an empty or malformed sbral therefore silently returns the string "cdr/sbral: not a valid sbral" instead of signaling an error. The sibling accessors car/sbral (line 32) and sbral-tree-lookup (line 49) raise properly. Worse, the returned string is itself accepted by a further cdr/sbral call (a string matches neither pair pattern, so it falls into the same else and is returned again), so a loop that pops one element too many, e.g. `(let loop ((s s)) (if (null? s) 'done (loop (cdr/sbral s))))`, never terminates instead of failing fast. Fix: `(else (error "cdr/sbral: not a valid sbral"))`.

**Evidence.** Ran with csi: `(import (aux fds sbral)) (print (cdr/sbral '())) (print (cdr/sbral 42))` prints `cdr/sbral: not a valid sbral` twice as a RETURN VALUE (no exception raised, exit code 0), while `(car/sbral '())` raises a condition as expected.

**Independent verification.** Read src/aux.fds.sbral.scm: line 38 is `(else error "cdr/sbral: not a valid sbral")` — unparenthesized, so the string is the clause's value; lines 32/49/58/64 use the parenthesized (error ...) form. Repro with /home/mnocentini/.local/bin/csi + scratch repo: `(print (cdr/sbral (list))) (print (cdr/sbral 42)) (print "still alive")` printed the message twice as a return value then "still alive", exit 0 (no exception). Also `(string? (cdr/sbral (list)))` → #t and `(equal? (cdr/sbral (cdr/sbral (list))) "cdr/sbral: not a valid sbral")` → #t, confirming the returned string re-enters the same else clause, so the claimed non-terminating pop loop is real. Contrast check: `(car/sbral (list))` raised "Error: car/sbral: not a valid sbral", exit 70. Tests in src/test/fds.sbral.scm never hit the invalid-input path, so nothing relies on the current behavior.

#### [MINOR] `aux.fds.sbral.scm:66` — update/sbral out-of-bounds and negative index give cryptic match error

update/sbral has no bounds clause: an index >= length recurses with (- i size) until the outer list is exhausted, and a negative index fails the `(< -1 i size)` test and recurses with an even more negative index the same way; in both cases match1/first then fails on '() with the generic message "match/first: uncaught value. ()" which points at neither update/sbral nor the offending index. sbral-ref (line 64) handles the same situation with a proper `(error "sbral-ref: index out of bounds" i)`. Fix: mirror sbral-ref — guard the recursive clause with `(<= size i)` and add an else clause raising `(error "update/sbral: index out of bounds" i)`; that also stops the pointless recursion on negative indices.

**Evidence.** Ran with csi: `(update/sbral 5 'x (list->sbral '(1 2)))` and `(update/sbral -1 'x (list->sbral '(1 2)))` both raise `match/first: uncaught value.\n\n()` whereas `(sbral-ref (list->sbral '(1 2)) 5)` raises the informative `sbral-ref: index out of bounds`.

**Independent verification.** Read /data/bitcoin/mnocentini-home/Developer/working-copies/aux.scm/src/aux.fds.sbral.scm lines 60-70: update/sbral (line 66) uses match1/first with a single pattern `(((,size . ,tree) . ,sbral*) sbral)` and no bounds guard; its else branch recurses with (- i size), so an index >= length (and any negative index, which fails `(< -1 i size)` and recurses ever more negative) eventually matches '() and match1/first raises its generic failure. sbral-ref (lines 60-64) by contrast guards the recursive clause with `(<= size i)` and has an explicit `(error "sbral-ref: index out of bounds" i)` clause.

Reproduced with the specified binaries (CHICKEN 6.0.1pre1, scratch repo on CHICKEN_REPOSITORY_PATH):
- `/home/mnocentini/.local/bin/csi -q -e '(import (aux fds sbral)) (print (update/sbral 5 (quote x) (list->sbral (list 1 2))))'` → `Error: match/first: uncaught value.\n\n()` (call history shows the recursion through update/sbral line 70 before the match failure), exit 70.
- Same for index -1: identical `match/first: uncaught value. ()` error, exit 70 — confirming the negative index walks the whole outer list before dying.
- `(sbral-ref (list->sbral '(1 2)) 5)` → `Error: sbral-ref: index out of bounds: 3`, the informative counterpart.
- Sanity check that in-bounds update works: `(update/sbral 1 'x (list->sbral '(1 2 3)))` → `((3 1 (x) (3)))`, correct.

Tests (src/test/fds.sbral.scm line 99) only exercise an in-bounds update; nothing documents the match error as intended behavior.

### aux.judy.scm

#### [MAJOR] `aux.judy.scm:23` — Judy arrays store raw C_words, dangling after any moving GC

JI stores the tagged Scheme value directly into the Judy array (*PValue = Value, line 23) and JG/JF/JN/JL/JP return the stored word as-is; the key Index is likewise the raw tagged word. CHICKEN's collector is a copying one: every heap-allocated object (strings, pairs, symbols, FLONUMS, bignums) moves on a collection, and the Judy array's C memory is invisible to the collector, so a stored heap value becomes a pointer into the dead semispace — a later judy-array-ref returns a corrupted object (silent garbage or segfault). Same for a bignum key, whose 'key' is a heap address that changes across GCs. Only fixnums/chars/booleans are safe. The existing test/judy.scm masks this: it stores literals ('(zero "hello"), symbols), refs them immediately, and its large tests use only fixnums. Fix: either enforce immediates (check (or (fixnum? v) ... ) in judy-array-set! and on keys, documenting the array as word->word), or keep values alive and stable by storing an index into a Scheme-side vector held in a GC root. Static review only — Judy.h is not installed here, so this cannot be executed.

**Evidence.** Code-level: aux.judy.scm JI (lines 17-32) does 'JLI(PValue, PJLArray, Index); *PValue = Value;' with Index/Value declared scheme-object (raw C_word), and JG (lines 34-49) returns 'res = *PValue' unchanged. Nothing registers these words with the GC (contrast chicken-timsort.c, which pins every retained C_word in CHICKEN_gc_root slots precisely because 'a C_word kept in a malloc'd struct silently becomes a pointer into the dead semispace').

**Independent verification.** Read /data/bitcoin/mnocentini-home/Developer/working-copies/aux.scm/src/aux.judy.scm: JI does '*PValue = Value' with Value declared scheme-object (raw C_word) and JG/JF/JN/JL/JP return '*PValue' verbatim; no GC root registration anywhere in the file. Judy.h is absent, so I compiled a minimal mimic of the exact JI/JG mechanism (store a scheme-object C_word in a C global invisible to the GC, fetch it back) with /home/mnocentini/.local/bin/csc -O2 (scratchpad/judy-mimic.scm). Output: 'fetch immediately: (hello 1000042) / equal immediately? #t', then after allocating 200k conses and two (gc #t): 'after GC, fetch gives: #(extras #(199564 #(#<procedure...> ... #<invalid immediate object> ...' followed by 'Error: unprintable block object encountered', exit 70 — the stored word now points into the moved semispace and dereferences to an unrelated object. test/judy.scm confirms heap values (lists, strings) and a symbol key are intended usage yet are only read back immediately after storing, and all large tests use fixnums only, masking the bug exactly as the reviewer said.

The letnondeterministic§ macro template (line 74) calls `pretty-print`, but (aux nondeterministic) imports only scheme, (chicken base), (chicken sort), srfi-69 and aux modules — never (chicken pretty-print). Because the identifier is unbound in the macro-definition environment, it resolves at the use site, so any client that imports (aux nondeterministic) without also importing (chicken pretty-print) gets a runtime 'unbound variable: pretty-print' from every letnondeterministic/letnondeterministic§ use. **Independent verification.** Read /data/bitcoin/mnocentini-home/Developer/working-copies/aux.scm/src/aux.nondeterministic.scm: the import list (lines 4-11) contains scheme, (chicken base), (chicken sort), srfi-69 and aux modules — no (chicken pretty-print) — while the letnondeterministic§ template (line 74) calls pretty-print. Reproduced with the specified binaries and scratch repository: `/home/mnocentini/.local/bin/csi -q -e '(import scheme (aux nondeterministic)) (print (letnondeterministic (cd cb ? m c) (cd (list 1 2))))'` => "Error: unbound variable: pretty-print", with the call history showing `(pretty-print105 (callcc106 (nondeterministic107 ...)))` inside the letnondeterministic§103 expansion — exactly the reviewer's trace. Adding (chicken pretty-print) to the client import list makes the identical expression succeed, printing `((left 0) (tried 5) (accepted 2) (ratio 0.4) ...)` then `(1 2)`. The shipped test src/test/letnondeterministic.scm line 2 imports (chicken pretty-print) itself, which is why the suite passes and masks the bug.

### aux.machine.env.scm

#### [MAJOR] `aux.machine.env.scm:28` — extend's equal? memo table poisons lookups under a finer same?: predicate

extend takes a same?: keyword (tested with eq? in test/machine.env.scm::test/extend-many), but the hidden cache created by letrec-tabled is always an equal? hash table (aux.tabling.scm:53, make-hash-table test: equal?). When same? is finer than equal? (e.g. eq?), the cache conflates keys the environment distinguishes, in both directions: (a) looking up an equal?-but-not-eq? alias FIRST caches (void) under the equal? key, after which looking up the genuinely bound eq? key returns the cached (void) — the binding becomes permanently unreachable; (b) looking up the bound key first caches its value, after which the alias wrongly answers the bound value instead of (void). The existing test only performs one alias miss on a fresh environment, so it never sees either direction. Fix: create the hidden table with the environment's same? (needs a hash for it) or, simpler, skip the cache (store: #f semantics) whenever same? is not equal?.

**Evidence.** Repro (csi): (define s "s") (define E ((extend E₀ same?: eq?) (cons s 1))) (E (string #\s)) => #<unspecified> (correct miss, now cached) then (E s) => #<unspecified> — the eq?-bound key answers void instead of 1. Reversed order on a fresh env: (E2 s) => 1, then (E2 (string #\s)) => 1 instead of (void).

**Independent verification.** Code reading: /data/bitcoin/mnocentini-home/Developer/working-copies/aux.scm/src/aux.machine.env.scm line 28 builds each extended environment with letrec-tabled, whose expansion (define-tabled in /data/bitcoin/mnocentini-home/Developer/working-copies/aux.scm/src/aux.tabling.scm line 53) always creates the hidden cache as (make-hash-table test: equal?) and keys it on `(,z)` — the environment's same?: predicate never reaches the cache. Executed repro (scratchpad/env-repro.scm with the specified csi and CHICKEN_REPOSITORY_PATH, against the -O2 compiled modules): (define s "s") (define E ((extend E₀ same?: eq?) (cons s 1))); direction (a): (E (string #\s)) => VOID (correct miss, cached under equal? key), then (E s) => VOID instead of 1 — the eq?-bound binding is permanently shadowed by the cached miss; direction (b) on a fresh env: (E2 s) => 1, then (E2 (string #\s)) => 1 instead of (void). Output verbatim: "alias lookup (fresh copy of \"s\"): VOID / bound key lookup (eq? s): VOID" and "bound key lookup first: 1 / alias lookup second: 1". Both failure directions the reviewer described reproduce exactly. The existing test (src/test/machine.env.scm test/extend-many, line 53-54) performs only a single alias miss on a fresh environment, so it passes while never exercising either direction — the same?: eq? feature is explicitly advertised and tested, so this is not documented/intended behavior. Nothing in the module's comments restricts same? to be coarser than equal?.

### chicken-timsort.c

#### [MAJOR] `chicken-timsort.c:160` — Comparator catching a nested sort's escape resumes the abandoned sort

When a comparator on the callback path (TIMSORT_USE_COMPARATOR) runs an inner timsort whose own comparator raises, and catches that exception with handle-exceptions and carries on, the outer sort can never complete. CHICKEN's saved callback continuations are strictly LIFO: the abandoned inner C_callback frame is still on the runtime's callback stack, and at the next callback return the runtime resumes the abandoned inner sort, whose comparator raises again -- this time OUTSIDE the handler that already caught it -- so the already-handled error propagates out of the outer sort and aborts it. The module's own unwind machinery anticipates this resumption (the `if (depth != 0) return;` guard at chicken-timsort.c:160 deliberately leaves abandoned inner root sets alive so a resumed frame does not read cleared roots), and the process state fully recovers afterwards (timsort-depth returns to 0, later sorts are correct), but the comparator contract in aux.timsort.scm:79-85 only forbids thread yields and continuation escapes -- it never states that catching a nested sort's escape is fatal, so today a caught error silently resurfaces outside its handler. Two latent issues in the same scenario: (1) timsort_roots_release (chicken-timsort.c:118) pops the head of timsort_inflight unconditionally, so if a sort could ever complete while an abandoned inner set sits above it on the stack, it would clear-and-free the abandoned sort's set (whose frames the runtime demonstrably resumes) and leak its own ob_item -- release should pop ctx->roots, not blindly the head; (2) if the resumed inner comparator does NOT raise the second time, the inner sort completes into an already-unwound dynamic-wind and the outer comparator's callback returns twice. Suggested fix: have timsort_comparator check `timsort_inflight != ctx->roots` before C_save/C_callback and fail the sort cleanly (the way res!=0 already does) instead of letting the runtime resume the abandoned frame; at minimum, extend the aux.timsort.scm:79-85 comparator-contract comment to forbid catching a nested sort's escape.

**Evidence.** Repro run with /home/mnocentini/.local/bin/csi: (define inner (timsort/gen (lambda (a b) (error "boom")) #f #f #f #t TIMSORT_USE_COMPARATOR)) (define outer (timsort/gen (lambda (a b) (handle-exceptions exn 'ok (inner '(3 1 2))) (< a b)) #f #f #f #t TIMSORT_USE_COMPARATOR)) then (handle-exceptions exn (list 'OUTER-DIED (msg exn)) (outer '(5 2 9 1 7))). Output: "outer sort result: (OUTER-DIED boom)" -- the inner 'boom' was caught by the comparator's own handle-exceptions (which returned 'ok), yet it re-surfaces and kills the outer sort; the csi call history shows the sequence: (error "boom") -> handler k56 returns #t -> timsort-unwind -> [cmp] (< a b) returns -> (error "boom") raised AGAIN from the resumed abandoned inner sort. Afterwards: "depth now: 0", "subsequent sort: (1 2 3)", "subsequent callback sort: (1 2 3)" -- recovery is clean. Control experiments: nested sorts that both complete normally, and comparators that raise straight out of a single sort, all work correctly (13-case battery all PASS).

**Independent verification.** Read chicken-timsort.c (guard `if (depth != 0) return;` at line 160; timsort_roots_release pops timsort_inflight head unconditionally at lines 116-130; timsort_comparator at 325-365 does C_save/C_callback with no inflight-vs-ctx->roots check) and aux.timsort.scm (contract comment at 79-85 forbids only thread yields and continuation escapes; timsort-unwind called from the dynamic-wind after-thunk at line 231). src/test/timsort.scm has no nesting/escape tests, so the scenario is untested, not intended behavior. Ran the repro with /home/mnocentini/.local/bin/csi against the scratch repo: (1) outer comparator catching an inner callback-sort's (error "boom") printed "[cmp] caught inner escape: boom", ran one more comparison, then the outer sort returned (OUTER-DIED boom) -- the already-caught error resurfaced outside its handler and aborted the outer sort; afterwards depth=0 and subsequent sorts (fast path and callback path) returned (1 2 3) correctly, exactly matching the reviewer's evidence. (2) Controls: nested sorts that both complete gave (1 2 5 7 9); a single raising comparator caught outside the sort recovered cleanly -- the failure is specific to catching a nested sort's escape. (3) Latent issue 2 reproduced and is worse than stated: an inner comparator that raises once then succeeds on resumption causes the abandoned inner sort to complete into the unwound dynamic-wind and the runtime aborts the whole process with "[panic] callback returned twice - execution terminated".

### aux.stream.sicp.scm

#### [MINOR] `aux.stream.sicp.scm:170` — stream:zip-with applies op to '() past finite streams

stream:zip-with never checks for stream end: it always builds (apply op (map stream:car streams)), and stream:car of an exhausted stream returns '() (its non-pair fallback). So zipping finite streams does not terminate at the shortest input; it silently feeds '() into op, which for the arithmetic series ops (series:+, series:-, series:×'s comb, …) surfaces as a confusing deep 'bad argument type - not a number' when the stream is consumed past the end. Since a stream may legitimately be finite ('()-terminated; list->stream produces them, and stream:take/stream:filter can end them), zip-with should terminate: add a guard that yields stream:empty when any input stream is null, e.g. via stream:dest/car+cdr or (cond ((any stream:null? streams) stream:empty) (else …)) inside Z. All-infinite uses (the whole series module) are unaffected.

**Evidence.** csi repro: ((list○take 5) ((stream:zip-with +) (list->stream '(1 2)) (list->stream '(10 20)))) => error 'bad argument type - not a number' (op is applied to '() '()) instead of yielding (11 22).

**Independent verification.** Read /data/bitcoin/mnocentini-home/Developer/working-copies/aux.scm/src/aux.stream.sicp.scm: stream:zip-with (lines 170-176) builds (apply op (map stream:car streams)) with no stream:null? guard, and stream:car (lines 31-36) returns its argument unchanged when non-pair, so an exhausted stream ('()) yields '() as an element. Ran the reviewer's exact repro under the specified csi/repository: /home/mnocentini/.local/bin/csi -q -e '(import (aux stream sicp)) (print ((list○take 5) ((stream:zip-with +) (list->stream (list 1 2)) (list->stream (list 10 20)))))' => "Error: (+) bad argument type - not a number: ()", with the call history pointing into aux.stream.sicp.scm:173-174 (Z's stream:cons body). Also verified the proposed fix causally: a scratchpad copy of Z with (cond ((any stream:null? streams) stream:empty) (else ...)) yields (11 22) for the two 2-element streams and (101 102 103) zipping a 3-element stream with stream:repeat, i.e. terminating at the shortest input.

#### [MINOR] `aux.stream.sicp.scm:203` — stream:scan wrong for non-commutative operators

stream:scan is built as (○ (stream:map (fapply op)) stream:prefixes), and stream:prefixes yields each prefix in REVERSED order (stream:tails conses the most-advanced tail onto the front of the streams list). So (apply op prefix) is called as (op aₙ aₙ₋₁ … a₀), and the first prefix gives the unary application (op a₀). For + and * (the only internal uses: series:log₂, taylor/π, numbers/*/…scan) this is coincidentally right, but for any non-commutative op the public stream:scan silently produces wrong values — even the first element ((- 100) = -100). The accumulating rewrite below fixes the order AND the O(n²) cost (see the separate optimization finding): keep an accumulator acc, emit a₀ first, then (op acc aᵢ) left-to-right.

**Evidence.** csi repro: ((list○take 4) ((stream:scan -) (list->stream '(100 1 2 3)))) => (-100 -99 -99 -100); a left-to-right scan gives (100 99 97 94). An accumulator-based scan written in the same session returns (100 99 97 94) and is equal? to the current scan on the first 50 partial sums with + (commutative case preserved).

**Independent verification.** Read /data/bitcoin/mnocentini-home/Developer/working-copies/aux.scm/src/aux.stream.sicp.scm lines 191-205: stream:tails conses (shift-first streams) onto the FRONT of the streams list each step, so stream:prefixes (map stream:car over each list) yields each prefix newest-element-first, and stream:scan = (○ (stream:map (fapply op)) stream:prefixes) applies op right-to-left. Reproduced with the compiled scratch repo and /home/mnocentini/.local/bin/csi:
  ((list○take 4) ((stream:scan -) (list->stream '(100 1 2 3)))) => (-100 -99 -99 -100)   [left scan would be (100 99 97 94)]
  ((list○take 4) (stream:prefixes (list->stream '(100 1 2 3)))) => ((100) (1 100) (2 1 100) (3 2 1 100))   [prefixes are reversed]
  ((list○take 4) ((stream:scan +) (list->stream '(100 1 2 3)))) => (100 101 103 106)   [commutative case coincidentally correct]
This matches the reviewer's repro exactly, including the first element (- 100) = -100. The module declares (module (aux stream sicp) *), so stream:scan and stream:prefixes are public exports. No test or comment documents the reversed order as intended: the only tests (src/test/stream.sicp.scm lines 288-295) and only in-repo callers (aux.stream.series.scm lines 171, 333, 336, 339, 427) use + and *, which mask the ordering.

#### [MINOR] `aux.stream.sicp.scm:267` — stream:enumerate-* leak raw untupled elements when input streams have unequal lengths

In stream:enumerate-upper/lower/all (and the same skeleton in stream:enumerate-weighted's B), the termination clauses `(else s)` / `(else r)` (e.g. lines 267-268, 278-279, 291-292, 314-315) splice the surviving input stream itself into the output when the other stream runs out. The output is supposed to be a stream of tuples, but past that point it contains the remaining RAW elements of the longer stream. Any downstream consumer that assumes tuples (e.g. a stream:filter predicate doing (car tuple), or the (apply weight scar) call inside enumerate-weighted's own interleave) then crashes or silently misclassifies. Correct behavior for the exhausted case is to emit the remaining cross-product tuples of the survivor against nothing — i.e. stream:empty — or, if one wants the rectangle enumeration to keep going, to keep tupling the survivor's elements with the already-seen prefix of the other stream; either way raw elements must never appear. Only equal-length finite inputs (and fully infinite inputs) avoid the bug.

**Evidence.** csi repro: (stream:->list (stream:enumerate-upper (list->stream '(1 2 3 4 5)) (list->stream '(1 2)))) → ((1 1) (1 2) (2 2) 3 4 5); (stream:->list (stream:enumerate-lower (list->stream '(1 2)) (list->stream '(1 2 3 4 5)))) → ((1 1) (2 1) (2 2) 3 4 5). The trailing 3 4 5 are bare numbers inside a stream of pairs; equal-length inputs ((1 2 3) × (1 2 3)) return only proper tuples.

**Independent verification.** Read /data/bitcoin/mnocentini-home/Developer/working-copies/aux.scm/src/aux.stream.sicp.scm:254-326: the (else s)/(else r) clauses at lines 267-268, 278-279, 291-292, 314-315 return the surviving raw input stream, exactly as claimed. Ran repro in scratchpad with the specified csi + CHICKEN_REPOSITORY_PATH: (stream:enumerate-upper (list->stream '(1 2 3 4 5)) (list->stream '(1 2))) → ((1 1) (1 2) (2 2) 3 4 5); lower 2x5 → ((1 1) (2 1) (2 2) 3 4 5); enumerate-all 3x2 → ((1 1) (1 2) (2 1) (2 2) (3 1) 3 (3 2)) (raw 3 interleaved MID-stream, not just as a suffix); (stream:enumerate-weighted +) 6x2 → ((1 1) (1 2) (2 2) 3 4 5 6). Downstream crash confirmed: (stream:filter (λ (t) (even? (car t)))) over the 5x2 upper output dies with "(car) bad argument type: 3". Equal-length 3x3 and infinite inputs produce only tuples, matching the claim's scoping.

#### [MINOR] `aux.stream.sicp.scm:338` — stream:map-*-pairs never terminate on finite streams

stream:map-consecutive-pairs (line 338) and stream:map-overlapping-pairs (line 346) have no end-of-stream check: their P loop unconditionally conses (func (stream:car s) (stream:cadr s)) onto (P ...). Since stream:car/stream:cadr of '() return '() (non-pairs pass through, aux.stream.sicp.scm lines 31-43), once a finite stream is exhausted func is applied to '() arguments. With a numeric func this raises 'bad argument type'; with a func that tolerates '() the result stream never becomes stream:null?, so stream:->list diverges. A 4-element stream fed to (stream:map-consecutive-pairs +) should yield (3 7) and stop; instead it crashes after producing them. Fix: destructure with stream:dest/car+cdr using the (s ∅) guard on both the stream and its cdr (as stream:filter/stream:take do), returning stream:empty when fewer than two elements remain. This also fixes the (commented-out, 'FAILING') Cesàro/Monte Carlo experiment's cesaro helper for any finite toss stream.

**Evidence.** csi repro: (stream:->list ((stream:map-consecutive-pairs +) (list->stream (list 1 2 3 4)))) => Error 'bad argument type - not a number' (instead of (3 7)); same error for odd length and for (stream:map-overlapping-pairs +) on the same finite stream.

**Independent verification.** Read src/aux.stream.sicp.scm lines 31-43 (stream:car/cdr pass '() through) and 338-352 (both P loops lack any end-of-stream guard, unlike stream:take-while at 332 and stream:§₂ at 356 which use the stream:dest/car+cdr (s ∅) guard). Ran with /home/mnocentini/.local/bin/csi against the scratch repo: (stream:->list ((stream:map-consecutive-pairs +) (list->stream '(1 2 3 4)))) => "bad argument type - not a number" (expected (3 7)); same for odd length and stream:map-overlapping-pairs. With a '()-tolerant func: ((list○take 3) ((stream:map-consecutive-pairs list) (list->stream '(1 2)))) => ((1 2) (() ()) (() ())), i.e. the result stream never becomes stream:null?, so ->list diverges. Infinite stream works: take 5 => (3 7 11 15 19). Only repo usage is the commented-out FAILING-TEST Cesàro block at src/test/stream.sicp.scm:612, which uses an infinite random stream.

### aux.commons.scm

#### [MINOR] `aux.commons.scm:73` — cond/λ re-evaluates the scrutinee expression once per clause

The cond/λ template substitutes the scrutinee expression v textually into every predicate test ((p? v)) and into the selected branch ((then v)) — lines 73-75. When the scrutinee is an expression rather than a variable, it is evaluated once per predicate tried plus once more in the chosen branch: side effects are duplicated and work is repeated. This is hit by real code: aux.machine.secd.scm line 251 writes (cond/λ (position i) ...) where (position i) is an O(|F|) list-index scan over the de Bruijn frame — it runs twice per identifier lookup (once for number?, once for the branch), and three times when falling to else. Fix: bind once — ((cond/λ v (p? then) ... (else otherwise)) (let1 (v* v) (cond ((p? v*) (then v*)) ... (else (otherwise v*))))).

**Evidence.** csi repro: (define count 0) (cond/λ (begin (set! count (add1 count)) 5) (number? add1) (else identity)) => count is 2 after a single-clause match; with more non-matching clauses before the match, count grows by one per clause. Real call site with an expression scrutinee: aux.machine.secd.scm:251 (cond/λ (position i) (number? (λ (p) (Id₊ p))) (else (K (Id₋ i)))).

**Independent verification.** Read /data/bitcoin/mnocentini-home/Developer/working-copies/aux.scm/src/aux.commons.scm:68-75 — the syntax-rules template expands (cond/λ v (p? then) ... (else otherwise)) to (cond ((p? v) (then v)) ... (else (otherwise v))), substituting the scrutinee expression textually into each predicate and the chosen branch. csi repro (compiled scratch repo, /home/mnocentini/.local/bin/csi): with (define count 0), (cond/λ (begin (set! count (add1 count)) 5) (number? add1) (else identity)) => result=6 count=2; with two non-matching clauses before the match => count=4; falling to else past one non-matching predicate => count=2 (the else branch evaluates v too, even under K, because arguments are evaluated before application). Real call site verified at /data/bitcoin/mnocentini-home/Developer/working-copies/aux.scm/src/aux.machine.secd.scm:251: (cond/λ (position i) ...) where position is a list-index scan over the frame, so it runs twice per identifier lookup. Tests in src/test/commons.scm:93-94 use only literal scrutinees, so nothing documents re-evaluation as intended.

#### [MINOR] `aux.commons.scm:102` — map/tree treats an empty () subtree as a leaf and applies f to it

map/tree's walker only recurses when (pair? (car sexp)); a '() sitting in car position fails that test and falls to the else branch, which applies f to '() as if it were a leaf. So ((map/tree add1) '((1) () 2)) raises 'not a number' instead of returning '((2) () 3)', and for an f that happens to accept '() the empty subtree is silently replaced by (f '()), corrupting the tree shape. Fix: add ((null? (car sexp)) (cons '() (T (cdr sexp)))) before the pair? clause (or change the pair? test to (or (pair? (car sexp)) (null? (car sexp)))), keeping the top-level ((null? sexp) '()) clause as is.

**Evidence.** csi: ((map/tree add1) '((1) () 2)) => Error: bad argument type - not a number (add1 applied to '()); ((map/tree add1) '((1) 2)) => ((2) 3) works. The walker's else clause is reached with (car sexp) = '() because (pair? '()) is #f.

**Independent verification.** Read /data/bitcoin/mnocentini-home/Developer/working-copies/aux.scm/src/aux.commons.scm lines 97-104: T's cond has ((null? sexp) '()), ((pair? (car sexp)) ...), else (f (car sexp)); since (pair? '()) is #f, a '() in car position reaches the else branch. Reproduced with the compiled module: csi -e '(import (aux commons)) (print ((map/tree add1) (quote ((1) () 2))))' fails with "Error: (+) bad argument type - not a number: ()" and call history pointing at aux.commons.scm:103 f, while ((map/tree add1) '((1) 2)) => ((2) 3) works. Silent-corruption half also reproduced: ((map/tree (lambda (x) (list x x))) '(() 1)) => ((() ()) (1 1)), i.e. the empty subtree was passed to f as a leaf instead of being preserved (expected (() (1 1)) under subtree semantics). The only test (src/test/commons.scm:68-69) uses a tree with no empty subtrees, and no comment or caller documents '()-as-leaf as intended.

#### [MINOR] `aux.commons.scm:121` — foldl1/lshift default H₀ identity always errors

foldl1/lshift declares #!key (H₀ identity) (line 121) but its body destructures H₀'s result with (let-values (((v tail) (H₀ (car l)))) ...) — it requires H₀ to return exactly TWO values (a seed and a tail-selector function). The default identity returns one value, so calling (foldl1/lshift f) without an explicit H₀ raises 'bad argument count - received 1 but expected 2' on every non-empty list. The default is unusable dead weight copied from foldl1 (where identity is correct). Fix: give it a compatible default, e.g. (λ (a) (values a cdr)) — which reproduces foldl1's behavior of folding f over the cdr with the first element as seed — or drop the default and make H₀ required.

**Evidence.** csi repro: ((foldl1/lshift +) '(1 2 3)) => Error: bad argument count - received 1 but expected 2 args: (#<procedure (a1090 v230 tail232)>). The sibling foldl1 with the same default works: ((foldl1 +) '(1 2 3)) => 6.

**Independent verification.** Read src/aux.commons.scm lines 113-126: foldl1/lshift declares #!key (H₀ identity) yet destructures (H₀ (car l)) with (let-values (((v tail) ...))), requiring two return values; identity returns one. Ran against the pre-compiled scratch repo with /home/mnocentini/.local/bin/csi: ((foldl1 +) '(1 2 3)) => 6, but ((foldl1/lshift +) '(1 2 3)) => "Error: bad argument count - received 1 but expected 2: #<procedure (a1090 v230 tail232)>", call history ending at aux.commons.scm:125 H₀ inside ##sys#call-with-values — exactly the reviewer's repro. Grep shows the sole caller, src/test/commons.scm:59, always passes an explicit H₀ ((λ (x) (values x cddr))), confirming the default path is never exercised and no test documents the default as intended.

#### [MINOR] `aux.commons.scm:144` — tuple/pred? crashes on empty tuple lists instead of vacuous #t

tuple/pred?'s worker P immediately evaluates (apply pred? (map car tuples)) before any null check, so calling the predicate with empty lists — ((tuple/pred? =) '() '()) — dies with 'bad argument type' from (car '()). The recursion itself already treats simultaneous exhaustion as success ((every null? cdrs) => #t), so the natural base case for all-empty input is #t (vacuous truth), and it matters for generic callers like the sort comparator use in test/microkanren-unionfind.scm:406 if fed empty rows. Fix: start P with a clause ((every null? tuples) #t) before the apply (mixed empty/non-empty then still falls through to #f via the existing every checks).

**Evidence.** csi: ((tuple/pred? =) '() '()) => Error: bad argument type (car of '()), whereas the same predicate on equal-length non-empty lists works and returns #t/#f as documented by test/commons.scm's test/tuple-pred cases.

**Independent verification.** Read /data/bitcoin/mnocentini-home/Developer/working-copies/aux.scm/src/aux.commons.scm:140-151: worker P evaluates (apply pred? (map car tuples)) before any null check, exactly as claimed. Ran with the scratch repo: /home/mnocentini/.local/bin/csi -q -e '(import (aux commons)) ...' — ((tuple/pred? =) (list 1 2) (list 1 2)) prints #t, while ((tuple/pred? =) (list) (list)) dies with "Error: (car) bad argument type: ()" (exit 70), call history pointing at aux.commons.scm:147-148. Checked README.md:280,307 and test/commons.scm:20-23: only non-empty/ragged cases are documented; no statement that empty input is out of domain. The recursion's own base case ((every null? cdrs) #t) already defines simultaneous exhaustion as #t, so vacuous #t for all-empty input is the consistent semantics and the proposed fix ((every null? tuples) #t) preserves the mixed-empty → #f path.

### aux.kanren.unionfind.scm

#### [MINOR] `aux.kanren.unionfind.scm:185` — reify/status skips reified indices after a repeated variable

The module header (line 10) says reified variables read as ▢₀, ▢₁, …, i.e. consecutive indices in order of first appearance, as in every miniKanren. But in `reify/status`, the R/S walker tests `(variable? v₀)`, which is true for *reified* records too: on the second occurrence of an already-reified fresh variable, `(↑ v)` returns its ▢ₙ root, `variable?` accepts it, and `(∪ v₀ (R/unionfind-size U))` unions it with a brand-new R record. The union is harmless for naming (the old root wins by rank), but it adds a parent edge, and since the *next* fresh variable's index is `(unionfind-edges U)`, that variable's index is bumped: `(run/with-symbols 5 (v w x) (≡ v w))` answers ((▢₀ ▢₀ ▢₂)) instead of ((▢₀ ▢₀ ▢₁)), and `(run/with-symbols 5 (v w x y) (≡ v w))` answers ((▢₀ ▢₀ ▢₂ ▢₃)). Answers stop being comparable with book output and with the module's own documented reading. The existing test suite never covers a repeated variable followed by another fresh one (its cases stop at (▢₀ ▢₀)), which is why it passes. Fix: in R/S change the dispatch line to `((variable/working? v₀) (∪ v₀ (R/unionfind-size U)))` and treat an already-reified root as done (fold it into the else). I verified the fix by replicating the reify pipeline against the exported primitives: term (v v x) then reifies to (▢₀ ▢₀ ▢₁) and (v w x v w) to (▢₀ ▢₁ ▢₂ ▢₀ ▢₁), with repeated-variable identity preserved.

**Evidence.** Repro with the compiled module: (run/with-symbols 5 (v w x) (≡ v w)) => ((▢₀ ▢₀ ▢₂)); (run/with-symbols 5 (v w x y) (≡ v w)) => ((▢₀ ▢₀ ▢₂ ▢₃)); control (run/with-symbols 5 (v w x) (≡ w x)) => ((▢₀ ▢₁ ▢₁)) shows the gap only opens after a repetition. Patched replica of reify/status (variable/working? instead of variable?) run in csi gives (▢₀ ▢₀ ▢₁) and (▢₀ ▢₁ ▢₂ ▢₀ ▢₁). Full microkanren-unionfind suite still passes as shipped (19 ran, 0 failed) because no test exercises this shape.

**Independent verification.** Code reading: in /data/bitcoin/mnocentini-home/Developer/working-copies/aux.scm/src/aux.kanren.unionfind.scm, `variable?` (line 108) is defined as `(or (variable/working? v) (variable/reified? v))`, and `reify/status`'s R/S walker (line 185) dispatches on `(variable? v₀)`, so on the second occurrence of an already-reified variable the walk returns its ▢ₙ reified root, which still passes `variable?` and is unioned with a brand-new R record built from `(unionfind-edges U)`; that spurious union adds an edge, so the next fresh variable's index skips. Repro against the compiled module (csi at /home/mnocentini/.local/bin/csi, scratch repo on CHICKEN_REPOSITORY_PATH): (run/with-symbols 5 (v w x) (≡ v w)) => ((▢₀ ▢₀ ▢₂)); (run/with-symbols 5 (v w x y) (≡ v w)) => ((▢₀ ▢₀ ▢₂ ▢₃)); control (run/with-symbols 5 (v w x) (≡ w x)) => ((▢₀ ▢₁ ▢₁)) — the gap opens only after a repetition, exactly as claimed. A replica of the reify pipeline with the proposed fix (dispatch on `variable/working?`) run through the exported primitives gives (▢₀ ▢₀ ▢₁) for term (v v x) and (▢₀ ▢₁ ▢₂ ▢₀ ▢₁) for (v w x v w), i.e. consecutive indices with co-reference preserved. The module header (line 10) documents "reified variables read as ▢₀, ▢₁, …", which the current output contradicts, and the test suite /data/bitcoin/mnocentini-home/Developer/working-copies/aux.scm/src/test/microkanren-unionfind.scm never combines a repeated variable with a subsequent fresh one (line 94 stops at (▢₀ ▢₀); line 128's cases have no repetition), which is why it passes as shipped.

#### [MINOR] `aux.kanren.unionfind.scm:269` — run/with-symbols #t mode returns reified-variable records, not symbols

Every mode of run/with-symbols pipes the answers through get/variable->symbol — except the #t (first-answer) mode at lines 269-270, which returns (stream:car α) raw. So (run/with-symbols 1 (v) ✓) gives (▢₀) where ▢₀ is the SYMBOL, while (run/with-symbols #t (v) ✓) gives a (term depth) list whose term contains variable/reified RECORDS: (equal? (car r) '▢₀) is #f, and any code comparing the #t-mode result against the symbols the other modes produce silently fails. The test suite only exercises #t mode with ground answers (#t), so the discrepancy is unpinned. Fix: ((lambda (α) (if (stream:null? α) #f (variable->symbol (stream:car α)))) ...), keeping #f for the empty stream.

**Evidence.** (run/with-symbols #t (v) ✓) => (▢₀ 0) where (symbol? (car r)) is #f (it is a variable/reified record that merely prints as ▢₀), while (run/with-symbols 1 (v) ✓) => (▢₀) with a real symbol. All other modes symbolize via get/variable->symbol (line 254-256, 267-272).

**Independent verification.** Read src/aux.kanren.unionfind.scm lines 245-272: the #t mode of run/with-symbols (lines 269-270) returns (stream:car α) raw, while the n and ↓ modes go through get/variable->symbol (stream:map variable->symbol). Ran a repro script under the scratch repo with /home/mnocentini/.local/bin/csi: (run/with-symbols 1 (v) ✓) => (▢₀) with (symbol? (car r)) = #t, but (run/with-symbols #t (v) ✓) => (▢₀ 0) with (symbol? (car rt)) = #f, (variable/reified? (car rt)) = #t, and (equal? (car r1) (car rt)) = #f — the record only prints as ▢₀ via the record printer (line 122). The module header (lines 10-11, 35-36) documents run/with-symbols as returning "a list of plain symbols" and says only the ↓ mode "keeps the (term depth) pairs", so #t mode violates the documented contract on both counts. Tests (test/microkanren-unionfind.scm lines 76-132) exercise #t mode only with ground/truthiness answers, so nothing pins the discrepancy.

### aux.kanren.micro.show.scm

#### [MINOR] `aux.kanren.micro.show.scm:109` — ->string/short collapses whitespace inside string literals

->string/short rebuilds the pretty-printed form character by character, replacing every RUN of whitespace with a single space — without tracking whether it is inside a string literal. Any answer containing a string with consecutive spaces (or other internal whitespace runs) is displayed with corrupted content in Markdown table cells, mermaid node labels and SXML cells: the report shows a value that is not the answer. Fix: walk the characters with an in-string flag (toggled on unescaped double quotes, honoring backslash escapes) and only collapse whitespace outside string literals.

**Evidence.** (import (aux kanren micro show)) (->string/short (list "a  b" 'sym)) => ("a b" sym) — the string content "a  b" is shown as "a b". A full report confirms it: (μkanren-run/markdown (q 3) (=° q "a  |  b")) renders the table cell as `"a \| b"` and the variables-view mermaid label as #quot;a | b#quot; (single spaces), while the cells-view label (which uses ->string/write) correctly shows "a  |  b".

**Independent verification.** Read /data/bitcoin/mnocentini-home/Developer/working-copies/aux.scm/src/aux.kanren.micro.show.scm:108-113: ->string/short walks the pretty-printed characters and collapses every whitespace run to one space with no in-string tracking, exactly as claimed. Reproduced with the specified csi: (->string/short (list "a  b" 'sym)) => "(\"a b\" sym)" while ->string/write gives "(\"a  b\" sym)". Full report repro (μkanren-run/markdown (q 3) (=° q "a  |  b")) renders the table cell as `"a \| b"` and the variables-view mermaid label as #quot;a | b#quot; (collapsed), while the cells-view label correctly shows #quot;a  |  b#quot; — the same answer displayed inconsistently within one report. ->string/short also feeds μkanren-markdown-code (line 118), the mermaid value node (line 245) and the SXML cell (line 437), so all three claimed surfaces are affected.

#### [MINOR] `aux.kanren.micro.show.scm:417` — Reports quote a μkanren-run form the macro rejects

μkanren-run/markdown, /markdown/table, /markdown/profiled, /sxml and /sxml/profiled (lines 417, 421, 424, 463, 468) embed `query: '(μkanren-run (v n) g ...)` in the generated report, but the exported μkanren-run macro only accepts a three-element head `(v n grounded)` (aux.kanren.micro.scm line 632). A reader who copies the query out of a report — the stated purpose of quoting it — gets 'no rule matches form' instead of the answers the report documents. Fix: either quote the real form `(μkanren-run (v n #f) g ...)`, or add a two-element-head rule to μkanren-run defaulting grounded to #f (which would also make the quoted form the more natural API).

**Evidence.** (display (μkanren-run/markdown (q 3) (=° q 1))) emits a ```scheme block containing (μkanren-run (q 3) (=° q 1)); evaluating exactly that with (aux kanren micro) imported gives: Error: during expansion of (μkanren-run ...) - no rule matches form: (μkanren-run (q 3) (=° q 1)).

**Independent verification.** Read /data/bitcoin/mnocentini-home/Developer/working-copies/aux.scm/src/aux.kanren.micro.show.scm lines 414-470: μkanren-run/markdown, /markdown/table, /markdown/profiled, /sxml and /sxml/profiled all embed query: '(μkanren-run (v n) g ...), and the comment at line 414-415 states the report "quotes the query itself". Read /data/bitcoin/mnocentini-home/Developer/working-copies/aux.scm/src/aux.kanren.micro.scm line 632: (define-syntax-rule (μkanren-run (v n grounded) g ...) ...) — only a three-element head. Reproduced with the specified binaries: (display (μkanren-run/markdown (q 3) (=° q 1))) emits a ```scheme block containing exactly (μkanren-run (q 3) (=° q 1)); evaluating that form with (aux kanren micro) imported yields "Error: during expansion of (μkanren-run ...) - no rule matches form: (μkanren-run (q 3) (=° q 1))", while the real form (μkanren-run (q 3 #f) (=° q 1)) expands fine (returns ((λ () 1))). Every real use in src/test/microkanren-untagged.scm uses the three-element head (q 1 #t), and shipped report src/test/testsuite-microkanren-show-suite.html line 75 already contains the invalid two-element form, so this is not intended behavior but report infidelity.

### aux.machine.sc.scm

#### [MINOR] `aux.machine.sc.scm:58` — SC curryfy rejects a combination in operator position

`curryfy` (lines 58-63) is `(foldl1 (λ (acc c) ...) H₀: (λ (i) (Id i)))`: the head of the s-expression always goes through `(Id i)`, which %checks `symbol?` and errors on a list. So `(curryfy '((p a) b))` raises "wrong field type", although an application whose operator is itself a combination is a perfectly legal curried combination — indeed it is exactly the shape the module's own printer emits (`(curryfy '(p a b))` prints as `((p a) b)`, which curryfy then cannot read back). Nested lists in operand position are handled (the `(list? c)` branch recurses), only the head is not; the SECD machine's curryfy handles both. Fix is one line: `H₀: (λ (i) (cond ((list? i) (curryfy i)) (else (Id i))))` (curryfy is a module-level binding, so the recursive reference is fine).

**Evidence.** Ran with the scratch csi: (sc:curryfy '(p a b)) → ((p a) b); (sc:curryfy '(p (m a) b)) → ((p (m a)) b); (sc:curryfy '((p a) b)) → error "wrong field type".

**Independent verification.** Read /data/bitcoin/mnocentini-home/Developer/working-copies/aux.scm/src/aux.machine.sc.scm (lines 32-63) and the foldl1 definition in src/aux.commons.scm lines 113-118: foldl1 applies H₀ to (car l), and H₀ is (λ (i) (Id i)) whose %check 'Id symbol? rejects any list. Reproduced with the scratch csi: (sc:curryfy '(p a b)) → ((p a) b); (sc:curryfy '(p (m a) b)) → ((p (m a)) b); (sc:curryfy '((p a) b)) → "ERROR: wrong field type args=((p a)) loc=Id" (call history: aux.commons.scm:118 H₀ → aux.machine.sc.scm:63 Id → %check → error). Same input through (aux machine secd): (secd:curryfy '((p a) b)) → ((p a) b), because secd's curryfy (aux.machine.secd.scm line 102) recurses on the rator. So the printer's own output ((p a) b) cannot be read back by SC curryfy while SECD's can.

### aux.hansei.scm

#### [MINOR] `aux.hansei.scm:142` — probcc-dfs returns nested list-of-lists instead of flat choices list

probcc-dfs maps M over choices, where M returns a LIST in both branches: `(list probpair)` for a leaf pair and `(apply append (probcc-dfs ...))` for a C pair. The top-level `(map M choices)` therefore yields a list of lists, e.g. ((((V #t) 1/2)) (((V #f) 1/2))), which is not a valid choices list (probcc-explore, probcc-normalize, mass computations all expect a flat list of ((V v) p) / ((C t) p) pairs). The C branch itself flattens the recursive result with `apply append`, proving the intended contract is a flat list — only the outermost call fails to flatten. Fix: return `(apply append (map M choices))` (i.e. use append-map) from probcc-dfs; the `apply append` inside the C branch then becomes just the recursive call. Note probcc-dfs is exported (module exports *) but is used nowhere in the repo and has no test, so the bug is latent.

**Evidence.** Ran with csi: `(probcc-dfs (probcc-reify/0 (τ (probcc-coin 1/2))))` prints `((((V #t) 1/2)) (((V #f) 1/2)))` — each element is a singleton list wrapping the pair, so e.g. summing (cadr e) over the result (the standard mass fold used elsewhere in the module) fails. grep over the whole repo shows no caller and no test exercising probcc-dfs.

**Independent verification.** Read /data/bitcoin/mnocentini-home/Developer/working-copies/aux.scm/src/aux.hansei.scm lines 142-146: probcc-dfs ends with `(map M choices)` where M returns `(list probpair)` in the leaf branch and `(apply append (probcc-dfs ...))` in the C branch — so every element of the top-level result is itself a list. Ran with /home/mnocentini/.local/bin/csi against the pre-compiled scratch repo: `(probcc-dfs (probcc-reify/0 (τ (probcc-coin 1/2))))` prints `((((V #t) 1/2)) (((V #f) 1/2)))` — exactly the reviewer's evidence; a two-coin model prints `((((V (#t #t)) 1/6) ((V (#t #f)) 1/3)) (((V (#f #t)) 1/6) ((V (#f #f)) 1/3)))` (inner levels flattened by the C-branch `apply append`, only the outermost level nested). The standard mass fold `(foldr (lambda (pp acc) (+ acc (cadr pp))) 0 ...)` raises an error on this shape ((cadr '(((V #t) 1/2))) fails), and `(probcc-explore +inf.0 <buggy result>)` raises an error too, since its match/first expects `(,slot ,pt)` pairs. A corrected variant using `(apply append (map M choices))` (with the C branch's inner apply-append dropped) returns flat lists whose masses sum to exactly 1 for both models, proving the intended flat-choices contract. grep over src/ shows probcc-dfs has no caller; src/test/testsuite-hanseitest.html merely reproduces the module source verbatim and no test in src/test/ exercises probcc-dfs.

### aux.lua.scm

#### [MINOR] `aux.lua.scm:24` — lua-make-empty: no NULL check and double-close via finalizer

lua-make-empty neither checks luaL_newstate for NULL (lua_close(NULL) on memory exhaustion is undefined) nor guards against double close: lua-close is exported (module exports *), so a user who closes a state explicitly leaves the finalizer to call lua_close on the same pointer again at GC time — undefined behavior/crash. Fix: raise an error on NULL, and route explicit closing through a wrapper that clears the record's state slot (and have the finalizer skip a cleared slot). Static review only — Lua headers are not installed here.

**Evidence.** aux.lua.scm lines 24-27: (let* ((state (luaL-newstate)) (state* (make-luaR state))) (set-finalizer! state* (o lua-close luaR-state))) — the finalizer closure always calls lua_close on whatever raw pointer the record holds, with no idempotence guard and no NULL check.

**Independent verification.** Read /data/bitcoin/mnocentini-home/Developer/working-copies/aux.scm/src/aux.lua.scm: lines 24-27 are exactly as quoted — (lua-make-empty) stores the raw luaL_newstate result in a luaR record with no NULL check, and set-finalizer! installs (o lua-close luaR-state), which unconditionally calls lua_close on whatever pointer the record holds; nothing ever clears the slot. Line 2 is (module (aux lua) *), so lua-close (raw foreign-lambda) is exported. Grepped src/ and src/test/: no tests and no callers of (aux lua) anywhere, so no intended-semantics evidence contradicts the claim. Since Lua headers are absent, I verified the FFI mechanics with a compiled stand-in (scratchpad/nulltest.scm, built with /home/mnocentini/.local/bin/csc -O2) that mirrors the record+finalizer structure with a fake newstate returning NULL. Output: "NULL pointer returned as: #f" / "fake_close called with (nil)" (finalizer fires at (gc #t) and passes NULL — with real lua_close that dereferences G(L)->mainthread and crashes; Lua's manual does not permit lua_close(NULL)) and, after an explicit close followed by GC, a second "fake_close called with (nil)" — demonstrating the double-close path: an explicit lua-close on (luaR-state r) is repeated by the finalizer on the same pointer at GC, i.e. double-free/UB with a real lua_State.

### aux.category.prob.scm

#### [MINOR] `aux.category.prob.scm:23` — Prob return uses inexact 1.0, destroying exact-rational distributions

The Haskell model documented in the header uses Rational (`return x = Prob [(x,1%1)]`), but `(define (return v) (list (cons v 1.0)))` wraps with float 1.0. Since >>= multiplies every outcome probability by the return's probability, one pass through return (or any bind, because bind's continuation typically ends in return) coerces an entirely exact distribution to floats. Consequences: (1) the monad right-identity law fails under equal? for exact distributions; (2) users who build distributions with exact rationals (e.g. (coin/tf 1/3)) silently lose exactness and pick up float rounding, so totals like the sum of probabilities become 1.0-ish floats instead of exactly 1. The fix is free: use exact 1, which is the multiplicative identity for both exact and inexact probabilities, so float workflows are unchanged while exact workflows stay exact.

**Evidence.** (p:return 'a) => ((a . 1.0)); (p:>>= '((a . 1/2) (b . 1/2)) p:return) => ((a . 0.5) (b . 0.5)) — right identity fails under equal?. Law harness output: 'prob right id (exact): FAIL' while all float-only prob laws pass. Also (>>= (coin/tf 1/3) (λ (x) (>>= (coin/tf 1/7) (λ (y) (return (and x y)))))) has total probability 1.0 (float) despite fully exact inputs.

**Independent verification.** Read /data/bitcoin/mnocentini-home/Developer/working-copies/aux.scm/src/aux.category.prob.scm: line 23 is `(define (return v) (list (cons v 1.0)))` while the header comment (lines 9-18) documents the Haskell model with `return x = Prob [(x,1%1)]` — an exact Rational. Ran the reviewer's repro with the specified csi (/home/mnocentini/.local/bin/csi, CHICKEN_REPOSITORY_PATH pointing at the scratch auxrepo):
  (p:return 'a) => ((a . 1.0))
  (p:>>= '((a . 1/2) (b . 1/2)) p:return) => ((a . 0.5) (b . 0.5))
  (equal? original that) => #f   ; right-identity law fails under equal? for exact distributions
  (>>= (coin/tf 1/3) (λ (x) (>>= (coin/tf 1/7) (λ (y) (return (and x y)))))) => ((#t . 0.0476190476190476) (#f . 0.952380952380952)); sum of probabilities = 1.0, (exact? sum) => #f — fully exact inputs became floats via a single pass through return.
Also verified the proposed fix is behavior-preserving for float workflows: (* 1 0.5) => 0.5 with (eqv? (* 1 0.5) (* 1.0 0.5)) => #t, and (* 1 1/3) => 1/3, so exact 1 is the multiplicative identity for both towers. No test suite or other module imports (aux category prob) (grep over src/ found only the .import.scm), so nothing depends on the inexact behavior.

### aux.tabling.scm

#### [MINOR] `aux.tabling.scm:26` — memoize! and define-tabled silently truncate multiple values

Both caching paths bind the computed result in a single-value context: hash-table-ref/store does `(let1 (v (apply missing key)) ...)` (line 26, used by memoize!) and define-tabled does `(set! v (begin body ...))` (line 59). In CHICKEN these silently keep only the FIRST value of a multiple-values return, so wrapping a (values ...) procedure corrupts it from the very first call — not just on cache hits — with no error at the wrapping site. Fix: capture with call-with-values into a list (or use receive) and re-deliver with (apply values ...), or explicitly reject multi-valued procedures with an error.

**Evidence.** (import (aux tabling)) (define (divmod a b) (values (quotient a b) (remainder a b))) (memoize! divmod) (call-with-values (lambda () (divmod 7 2)) list) => (3) instead of (3 1), already on the first call. Same with (define-tabled dm (lambda (a b) (values ...))): (call-with-values (lambda () (dm 7 2)) list) => (3).

**Independent verification.** Read /data/bitcoin/mnocentini-home/Developer/working-copies/aux.scm/src/aux.tabling.scm: line 26 `(let1 (v (apply missing key)) ...)` (used by memoize! via hash-table-ref/store) and line 59 `(set! v (begin body ...))` (define-tabled) both bind the result in a single-value context. Ran the reviewer's repro with the specified csi (/home/mnocentini/.local/bin/csi, CHICKEN_REPOSITORY_PATH pointing at the scratch auxrepo). Output:
plain: (3 1)
memoized 1st call: (3)
memoized 2nd call: (3)
tabled 1st call: (3)
tabled 2nd call: (3)
So (values 3 1) is truncated to 3 already on the first call, for both memoize! and define-tabled, exactly as claimed. Checked src/test/tabling.scm and README.md sections on (aux tabling): no test exercises multi-valued procedures and no documentation states a single-value restriction, so the truncation is silent and undocumented rather than intended.

### aux.schemer.seasoned.scm

#### [MINOR] `aux.schemer.seasoned.scm:153` — Non-symbol atoms crash every leftmost and rember1* variant

All four leftmost variants (leftmost/awkward :147, leftmost/awkward+letcc :164, leftmost/escape+explicit :181, leftmost/escape :194) and both rember1* variants (rember1*/letcc :210, rember1*/try :231) guard only with (symbol? first) and otherwise assume the element is a sublist and recurse into it. Any non-symbol atom — a number, string, boolean — positioned before the first symbol is therefore passed to a recursive call whose match/first has only () and (,a . ,d) patterns (or to (car ll)), raising 'match/first: uncaught value' / 'bad argument type' instead of skipping the atom or returning it. The book's originals dispatch on (atom? (car l)), so e.g. (leftmost '(1 a)) is well-defined there. Fix: replace the (symbol? first) tests with an atom? test where the function's contract is 'leftmost atom', or add an explicit atom clause that skips non-symbol atoms if only symbols are wanted. The test suites only ever use symbol atoms, which is why this never fires.

**Evidence.** Repro (csi): (leftmost/awkward '(1 a)), (leftmost/awkward+letcc '(1 a)), (leftmost/escape '(1 a)) and (leftmost/escape '("s" a)) all raise 'match/first: uncaught value' on the atom; (leftmost/escape+explicit '(1 a)) raises 'bad argument type' from (car 1). (rember1*/letcc 'x '(1 x 2)) and (rember1*/letcc 3 '(1 3 2)) raise the same match error.

**Independent verification.** Read /data/bitcoin/mnocentini-home/Developer/working-copies/aux.scm/src/aux.schemer.seasoned.scm:145-253 — all four leftmost variants and both rember1* variants guard only with (symbol? first)/(symbol? a) and recurse into anything else as if it were a list; match/first has only () and (,a . ,d) clauses. Ran a repro script under the scratch repo with /home/mnocentini/.local/bin/csi. Output: leftmost/awkward '(1 a) => EXN: match/first: uncaught value (1, ()); same for leftmost/awkward+letcc '(1 a), leftmost/escape '(1 a), leftmost/escape '("s" a), rember1*/letcc 'x '(1 x 2), rember1*/letcc 3 '(1 3 2), rember1*/try 'x '(1 x 2); leftmost/escape+explicit '(1 a) => EXN: bad argument type (1) from (car 1). Symbol-only sanity cases still work: leftmost/awkward '((a) b) => a, rember1*/letcc 'x '((a) x b) => ((a) b). Every specific repro the reviewer listed reproduces exactly as claimed.

### aux.category.maybe.scm

#### [MINOR] `aux.category.maybe.scm:6` — maybe return silently drops extra args; (return) crashes cryptically

(define (return . args) `(just . ,(car args))) takes a rest list where every other monad instance in the library defines unary (return v). Consequences: (return 1 2 3) silently yields (just . 1), discarding arguments instead of raising an arity error, and (return) fails with the unhelpful 'attempted rest argument access at index 0 but rest list length is 0' instead of a wrong-number-of-arguments error naming return. Change the signature to (define (return v) `(just . ,v)).

**Evidence.** Ran with csi: (return 1 2 3) => (just . 1) with no error; (return) => 'Error: attempted rest argument access at index 0 but rest list length is 0'. All other instances (list, identity, reader, writer, difflist, prob, ziplist) define unary return.

**Independent verification.** Read /data/bitcoin/mnocentini-home/Developer/working-copies/aux.scm/src/aux.category.maybe.scm line 6: code is exactly `(define (return . args) (quasiquote (just . ,(car args))))` as claimed. Reproduced with the specified csi (/home/mnocentini/.local/bin/csi, CHICKEN_REPOSITORY_PATH pointing at the scratch auxrepo): `(maybe:return 1 2 3)` printed `(just . 1)` with no error, and `(maybe:return)` failed with exit 70 and "Error: attempted rest argument access at index 0 but rest list length is 0: #<procedure (aux.category.maybe#return . args)>" — both behaviors match the claim verbatim. Grep over src/aux.category.*.scm confirms every other monad instance (identity, list, difflist, reader, writer, prob, ziplist) defines unary `(return v)`; maybe is the sole variadic one. The generic functor machinery in aux.category.monad.scm always calls M:return with exactly one argument (lines 28, 34-40, 67, 69, 81, 90, 99), so nothing relies on the rest-list form, and no test or comment (src/test/ has no maybe suite) documents variadic return as intended.

## Worthwhile refactorings and optimizations

Each suggestion below survived a maintainer-judge pass (25 others were rejected as churn, wrong, or negligible).

#### [optimization/major] `aux.base.scm:343` — foldr/concat-strings is O(n²), 950x slower than string-intersperse

(foldr string-append "" lst) copies the growing suffix once per element, giving quadratic character copying and heavy GC churn. Replacing the body with (string-intersperse lst "") (chicken string, already a dependency neighborhood) or (apply string-append lst) computes the total length once and copies each character once. Measured 0.957s → 0.001s on 20000 short strings. string-intersperse is the safer choice since it avoids apply's argument-list pressure on very long lists.

**Evidence.** csi benchmark, list of 20000 copies of "hello-world-string": (foldr/concat-strings ss) 0.957s CPU, 0.585s GC, 564 major GCs; (apply string-append ss) 0.001s; (string-intersperse ss "") 0.001s. Same resulting string-length.

**Judge.** Reproduced the benchmark with the specified csi: 0.96s CPU / 0.603s GC / 563 major GCs for the foldr version on 20000 short strings vs ~0.001s for both (string-intersperse lst "") and (apply string-append lst), with equal? results including the empty-list case. (chicken string) is already imported in aux.base.scm (line 12), so string-intersperse costs nothing dependency-wise, and the function is used on a real hot path — aux.anthropic.scm (lines 1154, 1488-1490) concatenates accumulated SSE streaming fragments with it, exactly the growing-input case where quadratic copying hurts. Clear, measured, low-risk win.

#### [optimization/major] `aux.base.scm:343` — foldr/concat-strings is quadratic in fragment count

foldr/concat-strings is (foldr string-append "" lst): each step copies the entire already-built suffix, so total work is O(total-length x fragment-count). Its hot callers in aux.anthropic.scm are the SSE accumulator (anthropic-sse-block->json, lines 1488-1490, once per content block over ALL of that block's text/thinking/partial_json/signature fragments -- a long streamed answer is thousands of text_delta fragments) and anthropic-response/text (line 1154). Replacing the body with (string-intersperse lst "") (chicken.string, one pass, one allocation) is a one-line change in aux.base that fixes every caller and is behavior-identical. Alternatively change only the aux.anthropic call sites.

**Evidence.** Measured (compiled module functions, csi 6.0.1pre1): concatenating 4000 fragments of 40 bytes (160 KB, a realistic long streamed block) x10: foldr/concat-strings 0.521s CPU (0.216s GC, 227 major GCs); (string-intersperse frags "") 0.001s CPU, 0 major GCs -- ~500x, and the gap grows linearly with delta count (quadratic total). Outputs string=?.

**Judge.** Verified against the compiled module: 4000 fragments x10 = 447ms via foldr/concat-strings vs 1ms via (string-intersperse lst ""), outputs string=? and both return "" for the empty list. The SSE accumulator really does push one list element per streamed delta, so the quadratic cost hits every long streamed block. foldr/concat-strings has no callers outside aux.anthropic, so the one-line aux.base.scm:343 replacement is safe and behavior-identical.

#### [optimization/major] `aux.category.prob.scm:40` — consolidate is O(n·d) per bind via linear find; hash-based version wins beyond ~60 distinct outcomes

consolidate scans the accumulator with (find ... acc) for every incoming pair, making each >>= O(pairs × distinct-values) and iterated binds super-quadratic overall. Since aux.base already documents the ~60-element crossover for exactly this scan-vs-srfi-69-table tradeoff (aux.base.scm:281, using length/>?), consolidate should adopt the same hybrid: keep the alist scan for small inputs, switch to an equal?-keyed srfi-69 table mapping value -> its (mutable) cell for large ones, still consing fresh cells in first-occurrence order so output order is preserved. Measured on an n-step ±1 random-walk distribution (n+1 distinct outcomes, 2(n+1) pairs per bind): compiled current code walk300/600/1200 = 0.145s/0.964s/7.271s (≈7x per doubling, super-quadratic); an INTERPRETED hash-based drop-in = 0.188s/0.725s/2.928s (clean 4x per doubling, the optimal asymptote given distribution growth). Despite running interpreted against -O2-compiled code it is already 2.5x faster at n=1200 and the gap widens; results verified equal to the original (same values, same order) at n=20.

**Evidence.** Benchmarks with /home/mnocentini/.local/bin/csi + (chicken time): original P:>>= walk 300: 0.145s, 600: 0.964s, 1200: 7.271s CPU. Hash-table consolidate (srfi-69, equal? keys, first-occurrence order kept): 300: 0.188s, 600: 0.725s, 1200: 2.928s CPU, and (equal? (walk-hash 20) (walk-orig 20)) => #t. Script: scratchpad/review6.scm.

**Judge.** Benchmark reproduced exactly: compiled original walk 300/600/1200 = 0.144s/0.967s/7.129s (super-quadratic, ~7.4x per doubling) vs interpreted hash drop-in 0.187s/0.722s/2.912s (clean 4x per doubling), already 2.4x faster at n=1200 despite the interpreter handicap, with equal? output at n=20. This is a genuine asymptotic fix to the hot path of every prob >>=, and the proposed hybrid (alist scan below ~60 elements, srfi-69 equal? table above) is the exact pattern already established in aux.base.scm's pairwise-different? (length/>? lst 60), so it adds no novel complexity to the codebase.

#### [optimization/major] `aux.fds.sbral.scm:72` — foldr/sbral does O(n log n) repeated sbral-ref instead of O(n) tree walk

foldr/sbral iterates i from n-1 down to 0 calling sbral-ref for every index, so each element costs a full O(log n) weighted-tree descent plus an outer-list scan. Every derived operation — sbral->list, map/sbral, filter/sbral, exists?/sbral — pays this O(n log n) cost, and exists?/sbral additionally keeps issuing sbral-ref calls after the predicate has already succeeded (only the predicate call is short-circuited by the `or`, not the traversal). A direct preorder walk of each (size . tree) entry visits elements in exactly the same order in O(n): `(define (tree->list tree acc) (if (null? (cdr tree)) (cons (car tree) acc) (cons (car tree) (tree->list (cadr tree) (tree->list (caddr tree) acc)))))` and folding that across the entries reproduces sbral->list; carrying an index counter through the walk supports the (i each) callbacks of map/filter/exists unchanged, and an escape continuation (letcc) or early-exit walk gives exists?/sbral true short-circuiting.

**Evidence.** Benchmarked with csi on the compiled module, N=100000: current sbral->list 0.154s / 0.152s CPU, 1493 minor GCs per run; direct preorder walk 0.031s / 0.029s CPU, 311 minor GCs — a 5x speedup with identical output (verified `equal?` on the two conversions before timing).

**Judge.** Correct and a clear win. foldr/sbral (aux.fds.sbral.scm:72-74) really does call sbral-ref for every index, each a full outer-list scan plus O(log n) weighted-tree descent, and all of sbral->list, map/sbral, filter/sbral, exists?/sbral go through it; exists?/sbral also keeps paying full refs after the predicate succeeds since only the `or` short-circuits. I re-ran the benchmark on the compiled module at N=100000: current sbral->list 0.154s/0.152s CPU with 1493 minor GCs vs 0.031s/0.030s with 311 minor GCs for the direct preorder walk — the claimed 5x speedup reproduces exactly, and I verified `equal?` output between the two conversions.
