The proposed tree walk visits elements in exactly the same front-first order, so the (i each) callback contract is preserved by threading an index. Asymptotic fix (O(n log n) → O(n)) in a module whose own comments call it the innermost loop of the substitution walk; well worth doing.

#### [optimization/major] `aux.fds.sbral.scm:42` — sbral-tree-lookup/sbral-ref: fixnum ops make lookup 1.7x faster

sbral-tree-lookup (lines 42-49) and sbral-ref (60-64) are documented as 'the innermost loop of the substitution walk' and already import (chicken fixnum) and use fx= for the i=0 test, but every other arithmetic op is generic: (quotient w 2), (- i 1), (- i 1 w/2), (<= i w/2), (< -1 i size), (<= size i), (- i size). All operands are fixnums by construction (sizes and indices). Replacing them with (fxshr w 1), fx-, fx<=, and (and (fx>= i 0) (fx< i size)) cuts lookup time by 42% in compiled -O2 code. Same change applies to sbral-tree-update (51-58).

**Evidence.** Benchmark compiled with csc -O2: 3,000,000 random sbral-ref calls on a 1000-element sbral. Local copy of current generic implementation: 2671/2683 ms; identical code with fxshr/fx-/fx<= : 1550/1552 ms (1.72x). The module's own cross-module sbral-ref: 2758/2765 ms. All three return identical results (checksummed).

**Judge.** Verified by compiling the benchmark with csc -O2: 3,000,000 sbral-ref calls on a 1000-element sbral take 2824/2831 ms with a local copy of the current generic code, 1634/1614 ms with fxshr/fx-/fx<= (1.73x), and the module's own sbral-ref measures 2810 ms; all checksums identical. All operands are fixnums by construction (tree sizes and indices), the module already imports (chicken fixnum) and uses fx= there, and the file's own comment declares this the innermost loop of the substitution walk, so the module has already committed to fixnum-level tuning here. A measured 42% cut in a documented hot path with a mechanical, local change is a clear win. Same reasoning covers sbral-tree-update.

#### [optimization/major] `aux.games.dice-of-doom.scm:133` — neighbors memoized on (pos board) though it depends only on (pos size)

`(define-tabled neighbors (lambda (pos board) ...))` keys its memo table on the whole board record, but the body reads nothing from the board except `(board-size board)`. Two consequences, both measured: (1) CHICKEN 6's equal?-hash does not descend past the first element of the key list — `(equal?-hash (list 0 board1))` = `(equal?-hash (list 0 board2))` = 517356 for two different boards — so all entries with the same pos land in one bucket and every lookup degrades to a linear scan with deep equal? over every board ever seen; (2) the table retains every board of every game forever (the boards are also each a fresh record per minimax node, so hits require deep equal? anyway). Since minimax/threatened call neighbors for every cell of every generated board, cost grows quadratically with the number of distinct boards. Fix while keeping the exported API: make `neighbors` a plain `(λ (pos board) (neighbors/size pos (board-size board)))` delegating to a `define-tabled neighbors/size` keyed on `(pos size)` (body unchanged, `hexnum` = `(* size size)`). The shipped 4x4/5x5 demo games stay short (0.04s/0.29s total) so current tests barely notice, but longer or deeper games hit the quadratic wall.

**Evidence.** Benchmarked with the scratch csi (scratchpad/neighbors-bench.scm), 2000 distinct 4x4 boards x 16 positions: module neighbors 12.28s CPU (misses) and 11.58s on the second, all-hit pass; the (pos size)-keyed variant: 0.154s. 100000 repeated-hit lookups against the filled table: 27.38s vs 0.47s (≈60x). Degenerate hashing confirmed directly: equal?-hash of (0 b1) and (0 b2) identical for different boards b1, b2, while the boards alone hash differently (333511 vs 393039) — so game-tree's key, whose FIRST element is the board, still disperses fine.

**Judge.** Verified end to end. The body of neighbors (aux.games.dice-of-doom.scm:133-152) reads nothing from the board but board-size (board-hexnum is size squared), yet define-tabled keys the equal? table on the whole (pos board) list. Reproduced the hash degeneracy on the scratch csi: (equal?-hash (list 0 b1)) = (equal?-hash (list 0 b2)) for distinct boards, so every entry with the same pos lands in one bucket and lookups degrade to linear equal? scans over all boards ever seen; the table also retains every board forever. Independent benchmark: 1000 distinct 4x4 boards x 16 positions took 1.67s with the module's neighbors (hit pass just as slow at 1.65s, confirming the scan) vs 0.112s for a (pos size)-keyed variant, and cost grows quadratically with distinct boards, consistent with the suggestion's 2000-board numbers. The wrapper fix keeps the (pos board) call shape used by the test suite (testsuite-dice-of-doom-suite calls it positionally) and shrinks the table to O(sizes) entries. Clear, measured performance and memory win with a tiny, safe change. One nit: the evidence's aside that game-tree's board-first key 'still disperses fine' is wrong ((b1 0) and (b2 0) also hash identically here), but that does not affect this suggestion's correctness.

#### [optimization/major] `aux.games.dice-of-doom.scm:95` — game-tree memo key defeats srfi-69 equal?-hash: bucket scans make tree construction quadratic

The memo key built at line 95 is (list board (players-rotation players) spare-dice first-move), stored in a (make-hash-table test: equal?). CHICKEN 6's srfi-69 equal?-hash is shallow: it stops mixing content after about one level of nesting ((equal?-hash '((1))) = (equal?-hash '((2)))), and a board record's distinguishing data (the cells) sits two-plus levels deep, so every board hashes to the same value (all boards give 519618107). The whole key therefore collides for every call that shares spare-dice and first-move, the table degenerates into a handful of huge buckets, and each game-tree call pays a linear scan of the bucket with a full equal? on (board rotation spare first) per probe — O(n) per memo operation, O(n^2) for building a tree of n distinct positions. The fix is to make the distinguishing data shallow in the key: splice the cell contents flat into the key list, e.g. (cons* spare-dice first-move (board-size board) (append rotation (apply append (vector->list (board-cells board))))), or pass a custom hash: that reads the cells, keeping equal? as the test. Note this is independent of (and additional to) the earlier-reported neighbors-memoized-on-(pos board) finding; the same shallow-hash mechanism is what makes that one catastrophic too, so its fix should also be checked against equal?-hash depth (keying neighbors on (pos size) is fine: both are fixnums at depth 1).

**Evidence.** Ran with csi 6.0.1pre1. (1) Hash collapse: (equal?-hash (mk i)) = 519618107 for boards with different cells; (equal?-hash (list board '(A B) 0 #t)) identical across distinct boards; (equal?-hash '((1))) = (equal?-hash '((2))) shows the depth limit. (2) Table micro-benchmark: inserting 10,000 distinct (list board '(A B) 0 #t) keys into an equal? hash-table takes 4.5s and re-looking them up 3.6s (~0.8 ms/op at only 10k entries, cost growing linearly per op). (3) End to end: forcing the full game tree of a 3x3 board (12,725 distinct nodes) with the shipped code takes 116s; a scratchpad rebuild of the game-tree/add-passing-move/attacking-moves trio with neighbors keyed on (pos size) takes 12.1s with the shipped key shape vs 10.3s with the flattened key (17% at this small size, gap widening quadratically with node count — a 4x4 tree is orders of magnitude larger). Scripts: scratchpad/hash-check.scm, scratchpad/dod-fix.scm, scratchpad/dod-fix2.scm, scratchpad/dod-prof4.scm.

**Judge.** Confirmed with the specified csi 6.0.1pre1: equal?-hash is depth-limited ((equal?-hash '((1))) = (equal?-hash '((2)))), every distinct board yields the same key hash, and inserting 5000 distinct memo keys takes ~1.3s with cost growing per-op — the memo table really is quadratic, which dominates game-tree construction. One important correction to the fix, though: the primary suggested remedy (splicing the cells flat into the key list) does NOT work — equal?-hash also truncates along the cdr chain after about three elements ('(1 2 3 4) and '(1 2 3 9) hash identically), so the flattened keys still all collide (verified: identical hashes, identical ~1.3s insert time). Only the suggestion's alternative — a custom hash: procedure that reads the board cells, keeping equal? as the test — fixes it: measured 153ms insert / 49ms lookup for the same 5000 keys (~8x at 5k entries, linear vs quadratic). The finding is correct and major and should be applied via the custom-hash route, not the key-flattening route.

#### [optimization/major] `aux.kanren.micro.scm:129` — Hoist length/sbral out of per-node walks: projection is 99% of runtime

μkanren-state-find (line 129) recomputes (length/sbral S) — an O(spine) fold over the sbral, ~334ns on a 600-var substitution — on every single call, and allocates two closures (var-deferred?, ref-var) per call. find is invoked once per NODE of a term by μkanren-state-find/value (line 164), μkanren-state-find/repr (line 173), μkanren-state-reify (line 185) and occur? (line 114), and μkanren-project runs three full-term walks per answer (find/value, then reify, then find/repr). Measured: producing 600 nat° answers takes 8ms of search but 907ms of projection — projection is ~99% of °->list runtime. The fix: factor the walk loop into a helper taking (S l) (e.g. μkanren-state-find/SL), keep μkanren-state-find as a wrapper computing l once, and hoist the (S, l) computation to the outer traversal in find/value, find/repr, occur?, and reify (in reify, resync S/l whenever the threaded state r changes, which happens only when a fresh var is reified). I implemented exactly this in a renamed copy of the module compiled with the same csc -O2; all 19 microkanren + 6 aggregation + 7 untagged suite tests pass on the variant. A deeper follow-up with the same motivation: cache the substitution length in the μkanren-state record, or store the total length at the head of the sbral spine in (aux fds sbral) so length/sbral is O(1) — that would also speed up μkanren-var-index/sbral and μkanren-update/sbral, which recompute it too (lines 85, 104-106). Note also that the find/value pre-pass in μkanren-project is NOT redundant: I tried removing it (reify/find-repr walk by themselves) and it got SLOWER (nats-600 621→690ms, appendo 59→77ms) because the pre-pass memoizes var-chain resolution for the two later traversals — do not remove it.

**Evidence.** Measured with /home/mnocentini/.local/bin/csi on compiled -O2 modules, medians of 3 runs. Microcosts on the 600th nat° state (S length 600): length/sbral 334ns/call, find(ground atom) 457ns/call (so the length fold is ~70% of it), find(bound var) 1.8µs/call. Phase split: states-only for take° 600 of nat°: 8ms; with projection (°->§): 907ms. A/B end-to-end after the hoist (variant also includes the small unify-dispatch change reported separately): nats-600 904→622ms (-31%), appendo all splits of a 150-list 79→60ms (-24%), ground-unify of two equal 3000-lists x30 74→66ms (-11%). Variant passes the full microkanren, aggregation and untagged suites (19+6+7, 0 failed).

**Judge.** Premise independently verified on the compiled modules: 600 nat° answers take 9ms of search vs 1030ms of projection, and μkanren-state-find (line 129) really does recompute (length/sbral S) and allocate two closures on every per-node call from find/value, find/repr, reify and occur?. The substitution S is fixed within each walk (only reify rebinds it, which the suggestion handles by resyncing), so hoisting (S,l) is semantically safe, the reviewer's A/B shows -24% to -31% end-to-end with all 32 suite tests passing, and the change is a local refactor of one function into a wrapper plus an (S l) helper. Clear measured win on the dominant cost of every query.

#### [optimization/major] `aux.kanren.micro.scm:469` — =° re-verifies every disequality in D per unification; 178x slowdown with 200 spectator constraints

Every successful =° that changes the state calls (μkanren-verify-D D s*), which performs a full trial unification (μkanren-verify-D+ → μkanren-state-unify/assoc) of EVERY prefix in the disequality store, even prefixes whose variables are untouched by this unification. Total cost is O(unifications × |D|). Measured: 400 unifications of fresh unrelated variables take 3ms with an empty D, 69ms with 25 spectator ≠° constraints, 136ms at 50, 264ms at 100, 535ms at 200 — perfectly linear in |D|, ~178x at 200. The information needed to do better is already computed and thrown away: post° at line 467 matches the unify result as ((,s* _)), discarding assocs, which lists exactly the variables bound by this unification. Since verify-D+ re-conses each surviving prefix re-keyed on its currently-unbound walked variables (d* from the trial unification), D entries are always keyed on live variables; indexing D by the variables occurring in each prefix (both sides of each pair) and re-verifying only the prefixes that mention a variable in assocs is sound and drops the per-=° cost from O(|D|) trial unifications to O(|touched|). The A and T stores are re-verified the same way per =° (μkanren-verify-A / μkanren-verify-T walk the whole store), but D dominates because each entry costs a trial unification.

**Evidence.** Benchmark (r3-bench1.scm, csi -O2-compiled modules): gen N spectator diseqs between fresh var pairs, then 400 unifications of other fresh vars, take 1 answer. N=0: 3ms; N=25: 69ms; N=50: 136ms; N=100: 264ms; N=200: 535ms. Linear fit ~2.67ms per diseq per 400 unifs, i.e. ~6.7µs per (diseq, unification) pair, versus ~7.5µs per unification total when D is empty.

**Judge.** Reproduced the benchmark with the pre-compiled modules: 400 unifications of unrelated fresh vars take 4ms with empty D, 71/139/274/544ms with 25/50/100/200 spectator diseqs — perfectly linear in |D|, ~136x at 200, matching the claim. The code confirms the mechanism: =°'s post° (aux.kanren.micro.scm:467-469) discards the assocs list that names exactly the variables bound, then μkanren-verify-D folds a full trial unification (μkanren-verify-D+ → μkanren-state-unify/assoc) over every prefix in D. The proposed fix is sound: verify-D+ (line 397) already re-keys each surviving prefix on the fresh assocs of its trial unification, so a prefix can only become violated when a variable occurring in it is bound, and skipping an untouched prefix at worst delays dropping an already-satisfied entry (benign, recheckable at reification). This is the standard faster-miniKanren-style variable indexing, and the module is a real engine (backs aux.tabling, carries D/A/T stores), so an asymptotic O(|D|)-per-unification cost is a genuine problem worth the added index complexity.

#### [optimization/major] `aux.nondeterministic.scm:40` — chooseD/chooseB backtracking cost grows linearly per exhausted choice (quadratic search)

Each rest-alternative thunk pushed by chooseD is `(τ (kk (chooseD (cdr choices) continue?)))` (line 40; chooseB is analogous at 47-52). When fail pops it, the recursive chooseD runs inside fail's dynamic context: its letcc capture and its pending `kk` application stack on top of all previously nested ones, so the k-th exhausted alternative pays O(k) — the whole enumeration of n choices is O(n²), and this dominates real searches regardless of pool discipline (chooseD and chooseB are equally affected). The same library's (aux continuation amb) ε avoids this by mutating the remaining-choices variable and re-entering via continuation-graft at a fixed capture site, staying linear; chooseD should be restructured the same way (capture kk once per choice list, mutate the tail, have the failure thunk graft straight back to that site) instead of re-invoking chooseD from within fail.

**Evidence.** Measured with csi: single chooseD over n all-failing choices inside letnondeterministic takes 22/87/388 ms for n=1000/2000/4000 (×4.0-4.5 per doubling => quadratic); all-accepted is the same (28/117/463 ms). Identical workload with (aux continuation amb): 0/1/0/2 ms for n=1000/2000/4000/8000 — linear and ~200x faster at n=4000.

**Judge.** Verified by running the benchmark against the compiled module: a single chooseD over n all-rejected choices takes 20/86/377 ms for n=1000/2000/4000 (~4.3x per doubling, clearly quadratic), while the identical workload on (aux continuation amb) is 0-1 ms up to n=8000 (linear). The diagnosis matches the code: fail invokes the rest-thunk, which evaluates (chooseD (cdr choices) continue?) inside fail's dynamic extent before kk fires, so each exhausted alternative re-nests the capture context. The proposed restructure is not speculative — amb's ε in aux.continuation.classic.scm (lines 120-127) already uses exactly the mutate-tail + fixed capture-site + graft pattern and stays linear, so there is a working in-repo template. Quadratic cost in the core enumeration loop of a nondeterminism library is a major, measured win; care is needed to preserve pool discipline (markD/cutD, chooseB's breadth-first append-right!) but that does not undermine the suggestion.

#### [optimization/major] `aux.stream.scm:105` — nats§ map§ tower is quadratic; primes§ inherits 21x slowdown

(define (nats§ s) (rec N (cons§ s (map§ add1 N)))) builds the naturals as a tower of map§ layers: the k-th element travels through k stacked, separately-memoized map§ streams, so producing n elements costs O(n²) time and O(n²) live promise cells (each intermediate layer is memoized and retained — a space leak on the module-level ℕ, which pins the whole tower forever). The equivalent direct recursion (define (nats§ s) (cons§ s (nats§ (add1 s)))) is O(n) and produces identical output (verified with equal? over take§ 100). primes§ (line 109) is built over (nats§ 2) and inherits the blowup, making it unusable beyond the test suite's 20 primes.

**Evidence.** Benchmarks with /home/mnocentini/.local/bin/csi (chicken time): draining nats§ — 2000 elems: 1.047s; 4000 elems: 3.871s CPU / 367 MiB max live heap (superlinear: 2x n -> 3.7x t). Linear variant — 4000 elems: 0.008s / 1.0 MiB (484x); 100000 elems: 0.203s. primes§: first 150 primes 20.14s CPU / 4.47s GC stock vs 0.968s over the linear nats (21x, gap grows with n). Outputs verified equal? for both nats and primes.

**Judge.** Verified by running /home/mnocentini/.local/bin/csi against the compiled scratch repo: the map§-tower nats§ (src/aux.stream.scm:105) is severely superlinear — draining 1000 elems took 48.8s and 2000 elems 450s, vs 0.45s for the direct recursion (define (nats§ s) (cons§ s (nats§ (add1 s)))) in the identical harness (~1000x at n=2000, worse than the suggestion claims on this box). Outputs verified equal? at 100 elements. primes§ inherits it exactly as claimed: 150 primes took 18.5s stock vs 0.87s over the linear nats (21x, matching the suggestion's measured factor). The space claim is also sound — module-level ℕ pins every memoized intermediate map§ layer forever. One-line fix, identical output, order-of-magnitude win: clearly worth doing.

#### [optimization/major] `aux.stream.series.scm:406` — prime? walks primes/eratosthenes; switching to primes/∞ is 22x faster

The exported prime? (lines 398-406) linearly walks primes/eratosthenes, the classic 'unfaithful sieve' whose n-th element costs a tower of n stacked stream:filter closures (O(n²/log n) forcing work plus a retained filter chain per prime). The module already defines primes/∞ (trial division by earlier primes up to sqrt), which produces the identical sequence far cheaper and with a flat memoized spine. Since prime? only reads the sequence until it meets or passes n, changing line 406 from (P primes/eratosthenes) to (P primes/∞) is a one-identifier change with identical results and a >20x speedup (growing with n), and it stops the eratosthenes filter tower from being forced (and permanently retained, being a module-level memoized stream) by every prime? caller.

**Evidence.** Benchmark with the compiled scratch repo, forcing the 2000th prime (17393) of each module-level stream from cold: primes/eratosthenes: 2.25s CPU, 0.183s GC (major), 106 major/17719 minor GCs, 3.1 MiB live heap. primes/∞: 0.103s CPU, 0 major/1399 minor GCs, 2.82 MiB. Same value returned; 22x faster.

**Judge.** Verified by running the compiled modules: forcing the 2000th prime of primes/eratosthenes takes 2.33s CPU (106 major GCs, 3.09 MiB heap) vs 0.107s (0 major GCs, 0.98 MiB) for primes/∞, both yielding 17389 — the same sequence, since primes/∞ (aux.stream.series.scm:350-359) does trial division producing exactly the primes. prime? (lines 398-406) only walks the stream until it meets or passes n, so replacing the single identifier at line 406 is behavior-preserving, ~22x faster, consistent with factorization (line 396) which already uses primes/∞, and avoids permanently retaining the eratosthenes filter tower in the module-level memoized stream. One-line change, measured major win. (Minor nit: the evidence quotes the 2000th prime as 17393; it is 17389 — but both streams agree and the timings reproduce.)

#### [optimization/major] `aux.stream.sicp.scm:203` — stream:scan is O(n²) via prefixes; accumulator makes it O(n)

stream:scan re-applies op to the whole prefix for every element (via stream:tails/stream:prefixes, which also retain a growing list of every previous tail): n partial sums cost Θ(n²) applications plus a fresh list allocation per element. An accumulating scan — emit a₀, then thread acc with (op acc aᵢ) — is Θ(n), drops the retained tails, and as a bonus fixes the non-commutative order bug reported separately. This hits real module-level streams: series:log₂, taylor/π (convergence-acceleration series), numbers/triangular/+scan, numbers/fibs/+scan, numbers/fibs/∙scan. Suggested replacement (keeping stream:prefixes/stream:tails as they are for other users): (define (stream:scan op) (λ (s) (delay-force (stream:dest/car+cdr (s ∅) ((a d) (letrec ((S (λ§ (acc s) (stream:dest/car+cdr (s ∅) ((b d2) (let ((acc (op acc b))) (stream:cons acc (S acc d2)))))))) (stream:cons a (S a d))))))).

**Evidence.** Measured with csi + (chicken time), 3000 partial sums of (series:from 0): current ((stream:scan +)) 1.03s CPU, 43 major/8330 minor GCs, 9.1M mutations; accumulator version 0.016s CPU, 0 major/165 minor GCs, 124k mutations — 64x faster and asymptotically better. Outputs equal? on the first 50 elements with +.

**Judge.** Verified by running it: 3000 partial sums of (series:from 0) take 1.04s CPU / 43 major GCs / 9.1M mutations with the current prefixes-based scan vs 0.02s / 0 major GCs / 133k mutations with the proposed accumulator (52x, and asymptotically O(n) vs O(n²)); outputs are equal? on the first 50 elements with +. The quadratic cost is real (each element re-applies op to the whole reversed prefix and stream:tails retains every previous tail list), and it taxes five module-level streams in aux.stream.series.scm (series:log₂ at line 171, taylor/π at 427, numbers/triangular/+scan, numbers/fibs/+scan, numbers/fibs/∙scan at 332-339), all of which use commutative + or * so behavior there is unchanged. Note the observable change for non-commutative ops: the old scan applies op to the reversed prefix (op a₂ a₁ a₀), the new one folds left in stream order — arguably a fix, but worth a line in the module header's list of deviations. Clear major win.

#### [optimization/minor] `aux.anthropic.scm:392` — utf8-length: use string->utf8 byte length

anthropic-json/utf8-length walks the string char by char summing per-codepoint byte widths. CHICKEN 6 strings are UTF-8 internally, so (bytevector-length (string->utf8 s)) gives the same answer via one primitive copy, ~120x faster. It runs on EVERY anthropic-json/parse call (line 404), i.e. on every response body, every SSE data frame and every stub-recorded request, so a 1 MB response spends ~36 ms here for ~0.3 ms of real work. (scheme base) is already imported; drop-in replacement: (define (anthropic-json/utf8-length s) (bytevector-length (string->utf8 s))).

**Evidence.** Measured x10 on a 1 MB string (module fn compiled -O2): loop 0.361s CPU vs string->utf8 0.003s. Results = on both pure-ASCII and mixed 2-byte-char strings (agree ascii: #t, agree mixed: #t).

**Judge.** Verified: (bytevector-length (string->utf8 s)) agrees with the hand loop on ASCII, 2-byte and 4-byte (emoji) inputs, and measured 4380ms vs 2ms x10 on a 1MB string. (scheme base) is already imported. It runs on every anthropic-json/parse (every response body and SSE frame), and the change is also a clarity win: an 8-line codepoint-width loop becomes the standard one-line primitive.

#### [optimization/minor] `aux.kanren.micro.scm:159` — match/non-overlapping in unify's pair case costs ~10-17% of unification

The pair-vs-pair clause of μkanren-state-unify (lines 159-161) dispatches on the result of the recursive car-unification through match/non-overlapping, which per cons cell allocates a failure-continuation thunk per clause plus a dmatch-pkg record per matching clause, and evaluates ALL clauses to check for ambiguity (dmatch-aux/dmatch-run-a-thunk in aux.base.scm lines 51-75). The result is only ever a 2-list or #f, so the checked-ambiguity machinery buys nothing here. Replacing it with (let1 (r (μkanren-state-unify (car u*) (car v*) s assocs)) (and r (μkanren-state-unify (cdr u*) (cdr v*) (car r) (cadr r)))) is behavior-identical (unify returns (s* assocs*) or #f) and measurably faster on unification-heavy workloads. The same pattern appears on colder paths (μkanren-subsumed? line 256, μkanren-verify-D+ line 397) where it is fine to keep for its checking value.

**Evidence.** A/B with compiled -O2 modules: unifying two equal 3000-element ground lists 30 times: 75ms baseline vs 62ms with only this change (-17%); in the final combined variant 74→66ms (-11%). appendo-splits-150 also improved 81→80ms with this change alone (most of its time is projection). Full microkanren suite (19 tests) passes with the change.

**Judge.** Independently A/B'd: compiled a baseline and a variant copy of the module with the same csc -s -O2 and measured unification of two 3000-element ground lists x30 at 74-75ms vs 66-69ms (~10% faster) with only this change. The replacement is provably behavior-identical (μkanren-state-unify returns a 2-list or #f, and aux.base.scm's dmatch-run-a-thunk confirms the per-cell pkg-record/thunk allocation and ambiguity check being paid for nothing here). It is the hottest path in the engine, the change is three lines, and the suggestion sensibly keeps the checked match on cold paths where its ambiguity checking has value.

#### [optimization/minor] `aux.kanren.unionfind.scm:177` — reify/status is O(k²): full-table walk per fresh variable via R/unionfind-size

reify/status names each fresh variable by calling (R/unionfind-size U) (line 185), and R/unionfind-size (lines 173-175) computes unionfind-edges, which walks the WHOLE reification hash table to count non-self-loop links. Since each reified variable adds edges, reifying an answer term with k distinct fresh variables costs k table walks over a growing table — quadratic in k, and reify/var pays it once per answer. Replacing the per-variable table walk with a local counter incremented at each (∪ v₀ (R '▢ n)) is a two-line change, makes reification linear, and as a bonus makes the indices dense 0,1,2,... — which also fixes the separately-reported 'reify/status skips reified indices after a repeated variable' bug, since the counter only advances when a variable is actually reified.

**Evidence.** Benchmark reifying a list of k distinct V/gensym variables with the exported reify/status vs. a counter-based variant doing the same union-find writes (csi 6.0.1pre1, -O2 compiled module): k=1000: 154 ms vs 8 ms; k=2000: 599 ms vs 17 ms; k=4000: 2315 ms vs 31 ms. Current version quadruples per doubling (quadratic); counter version is linear, 75x faster at k=4000.

**Judge.** Verified by running the compiled module: benchmark reproduced the claimed quadratic scaling (159/607/2419 ms at k=1000/2000/4000, quadrupling per doubling). unionfind-edges provably walks the whole π hash table per reified variable. The counter fix is small, makes reification linear, and I confirmed the bonus claim: current code reifies (a b a c) as (▢₀ ▢₁ ▢₀ ▢₃), skipping index 2, while existing tests expect dense indices and none depends on skipping — so dense 0,1,2 indexing is both standard miniKanren behavior and a bug fix. One caveat for the implementer: the variable? branch at line 185 also unions already-reified variables (that re-union is what causes the skip), so the fix must guard with variable/working? so the counter only advances on genuinely fresh variables — which is exactly the behavior the suggestion describes. Correct diagnosis, measured 75x win, two-line-scale change: clear net win.

#### [optimization/minor] `aux.stream.series.scm:425` — series:log₂/taylor-π summands stack a (stream:map -) per term: quadratic; carrying the sign is linear

Both series:log₂ (lines 168-171) and taylor/π (lines 424-427) build their alternating summands as (stream:cons (⁻¹ n) ((stream:map -) (summands (next n)))): term k of the resulting stream is buried under k nested (stream:map -) wrappers, so forcing the first n terms costs O(n²) promise forcings and allocations — the same anti-pattern as the already-reported nats§ tower, but an independent instance that survives even after stream:scan is fixed. Rewriting the generator to carry the sign explicitly, e.g. (letrec ((S (λ§ (n sign) (stream:cons (* sign (⁻¹ n)) (S (+ n 2) (- sign)))))) (S 1 1)), produces the identical stream in O(n).

**Evidence.** Compiled benchmark (csc -O2) of an exact replica of the taylor/π summands: forcing term 2000 takes 2.17s CPU (161 major GCs), term 4000 takes 6.72s (466 major GCs) — super-linear scaling — while the sign-carrying variant reaches term 4000 (same value 1/8001) in 0.002s: ~3300x faster at n=4000 and growing.

**Judge.** Verified with an exact replica of the taylor/π summands generator run against the compiled (aux stream sicp): forcing term 2000 of the nested-map tower took 2.28s CPU (178 major GCs) and term 4000 took a further 6.9s (275 major GCs) — clearly super-linear — while the proposed sign-carrying generator produced the identical value (1/8001 at term 4000) in 0.011s with zero major GCs. The same anti-pattern is at series:log₂ (lines 168-171) and taylor/π (lines 424-427); the rewrite is small, local, and yields an identical stream. Real quadratic-to-linear fix, worth doing in both places.

#### [optimization/minor] `aux.stream.sicp.scm:74` — stream:dest/car+cdr forces the stream three times per node

Every expansion of stream:dest/car+cdr calls stream:null?, stream:car and stream:cdr, each of which is a ○-composed closure that independently walks the whole promise chain with stream:force* — three traversals plus compose-call overhead per destructured node (the source comment on this line already flags the double force). Since virtually every combinator in (aux stream sicp) and every series operation in (aux stream series) goes through this macro, it taxes all stream consumption. Fix inside the macro: bind the forced value once — (let ((f (stream:force* s))) (cond ((null? f) sexp) (else (let ((a (if (pair? f) (car f) f)) (d (if (pair? f) (cdr f) f))) body)))) — preserving the existing non-pair fallback semantics of stream:car/stream:cdr; stream:force* is defined in the same module so it is visible at every expansion site.

**Evidence.** Measured with csi + (chicken time) on a fully memoized (series:from 0): walking 200k nodes x10 in dest style (stream:null? + stream:car + stream:cdr, mirroring the macro expansion) takes 5.59s CPU / 28.3M mutations / 57k minor GCs; the single-force walk takes 3.11s CPU / 16.4M mutations / 35k minor GCs — 1.8x on the access path with identical results (verified equal). Compiled -O2 code should keep a similar ratio since the cost is the redundant promise-chain walks and closure calls.

**Judge.** Verified by running it: walking 200k memoized nodes x10 in the macro's current style (stream:null? + stream:car + stream:cdr, three independent stream:force* promise-chain walks through ○-composed closures) takes 5.63s CPU / 28.4M mutations vs 1.25s / 274k mutations for the single-force version — 4.5x on this box, even better than the claimed 1.8x, with identical sums. The non-pair fallback semantics are preserved exactly (checked: stream:car/cdr of a forced non-pair return the value itself, as does the proposed (if (pair? f) (car f) f) form). The macro sits on the hot path of essentially every combinator in (aux stream sicp) and every operation in (aux stream series), the source comment at line 74 already flags the redundant forcing, stream:force* is exported (module exports *) so the expansion resolves at external call sites too, and the fix also drops the current triple evaluation of the s expression. Clear win, small and local.

#### [refactoring/major] `aux.kanren.micro.scm:609` — Recursive relation without fresh vars diverges at goal construction

There is no inverse-eta delay anywhere in the goal constructors: and°/or° expand to calls of the FUNCTIONS andª/orª, whose arguments are evaluated when the goal is built, and (fresh° () body ...) (line 499) expands to plain (and° body ...) with no suspension. The only suspension point is freshª with at least one variable (its (δ (g s*)) at line 461 delays application, and (f α) delays construction of the body until application). Consequently (define-relation (ones° x) (or° (=° x 1) (ones° x))) or the same body under (fresh° () ...) loops forever the moment the relation is CALLED — before any state is involved — while the identical relation guarded by a dummy (fresh° (ignore) ...) works. This is a silent footgun: the divergence gives no error, just a hang. Two concrete, tested fixes: (a) make define-relation (line 609) eta-delay its body: (define (name arg ...) (λ (s) (δ ((and° g ...) s)))) — I verified this pattern terminates and yields the right answers for the unguarded recursive relation ((1 1 1) for take° 3 of ones°); caveat: the extra promise per relation call shifts interleaving positions, so answer ORDER within disjunctions and the profiled suspension counts can change, which existing tests may notice; or (b) zero-risk alternative: make only the currently-useless (fresh° () body ...) clause suspend: ((fresh° () body ...) (μ s (δ ((and° body ...) s)))) — no existing test or source file uses (fresh° ()) today, and it gives users an explicit suspension form matching the documented "guard recursion with fresh°" idiom.

**Evidence.** Ran: a probe file whose relation used (fresh° () (or° (=° x 1) (ones° x))) hung the whole csi run (timeout 60s, exit 124) at goal-construction time; the same probes with (fresh° (ignore) ...) produce (1 2 1 2 1 2) for the interleaved take° 6. The eta-delay fix verified live: (define (ones° x) (λ (s) (δ ((or° (=° x 1) (ones° x)) s)))) then (°->list/ground (take° 3 (fresh° (q) (ones° q)))) => (1 1 1).

**Judge.** Reproduced live: (define-relation (ones° x) (or° (=° x 1) (ones° x))) hangs the moment the relation is called (timeout, no error), while the eta-delayed form terminates with (1 1 1) — a silent divergence with no diagnostic is a real footgun, and canonical microKanren's define-relation includes exactly this inverse-eta delay. Verified that (fresh° ()) is used nowhere in the repo, so fix (b) (make the empty-fresh clause suspend) is zero-risk and turns a currently useless clause into the documented escape hatch; fix (a) (eta-delay in define-relation) is the more principled fix but its answer-order shifts need a test-suite pass first. Either way, worth doing.

#### [refactoring/major] `aux.machine.secd.scm:351` — →/compiled is →/compiled⁺ restricted: five clauses duplicated verbatim

→/compiled (lines 351-387) is clause-for-clause a copy of →/compiled⁺ (444-482) restricted to the instructions that plain compile emits: the Load, Position, Closure and Test clauses are textually identical, and →/compiled's Apply clause is exactly PA's closure₊ and else branches ((PA f y S₊ E C₊ D) builds the same (make-status '() (cons y E₁) C₁ (make-status S₊ E C₊ D)) / (make-status (cons (f y) S₊) E C₊ D)). Since compile never emits Enter/Exit/Position&Apply/Jump and its programs never put program-closures or status-appenders on the stack, →/compiled can be defined as (define →/compiled →/compiled⁺) — or, if the distinct 'no transition for' error message on the ⁺-only instructions is worth keeping, both transitions can share one clause-dispatch helper parameterised by the Apply handler. This removes ~35 duplicated lines and is a different duplication from the already-reported dump-popping clause (that one is the (null? C) prologue; this is the whole instruction switch). The test suite itself already runs →/compiled⁺ on compile output (test/machine.secd.scm:205) and expects the same answer.

**Evidence.** Verified with csi: for ((λ (f) (λ (x) (f (f x)))) ² three) compiled with plain compile, the rtc traces of →/compiled and →/compiled⁺ are element-wise identical (14 states each, equal? #t); for the Y-combinator length program over a 3-element list (exercising Closure, Test, Apply and dump popping), both traces are identical too (94 states each, equal? #t).

**Judge.** Verified by running both machines from the scratch repo: on plain-compile programs (control₁ and the Y-combinator length over a 3-element list) the rtc traces of →/compiled and →/compiled⁺ are element-wise identical — 14 and 94 states, exactly as the evidence claims. The Load/Position/Closure/Test clauses are textually identical, and PA's closure₊ and else branches are exactly →/compiled's Apply clause; program-closures and status-appenders only arise from Jump/⁺-instructions plain compile never emits, so the restriction claim is sound and the test suite (machine.secd.scm:205) already runs →/compiled⁺ on compile output expecting the same answer. Keeping ~35 verbatim-duplicated transition-switch lines in sync is a real divergence hazard (this port has already had fixes applied to it), so deduplicating is a clear net win. One caveat for a pedagogical port: the bare alias (define →/compiled →/compiled⁺) erases the plain machine's 'no transition for' rejection of ⁺-only instructions, which the in-code comment presents deliberately ('as with the old cases, this machine has no rule...'); prefer the suggestion's second option — a shared clause-dispatch helper parameterised by the Apply handler — which removes the duplication while keeping both machines' identities and error behavior.

#### [refactoring/minor] `aux.anthropic.scm:1082` — Request options mirrored across four keyword lists

The eleven request options (model, max-tokens, system, tools, tool-choice, disable-parallel, thinking, effort, stop-sequences, stream, extra) are spelled out four times: as #!key formals of anthropic-request/body (1082-1093), anthropic/messages (1363-1374), anthropic/stream (1624-1637) and anthropic/converse (1724-1739), plus three full forwarding call sites (1377-1382, 1640-1646, 1756-1762) that re-list every keyword. Adding one API option today (as `effort` evidently was) means editing seven spots in lock-step, and a keyword silently dropped from one forwarding list would just use the default -- no error. Concrete change: make anthropic/messages and anthropic/stream take `messages #!rest options`, pull out their own non-request keys with get-keyword (betas:, and for stream on-event:/on-text:), and forward with (apply anthropic-request/body messages options) -- exactly the pattern anthropic/ask already uses at line 1388-1390 to forward kwargs to anthropic/messages. anthropic/converse keeps explicit keys it actually reads (tools, max-iterations, max-pauses, around, on-response) and forwards the rest the same way. This collapses the option surface to one definition site (anthropic-request/body) without changing any call site.

**Evidence.** Code-level: diff lines 1082-1093 vs 1363-1374 vs 1624-1637 vs 1724-1739 -- the keyword lists are identical member for member (stream: is implicit on the stream path); the forwarding blocks at 1377-1382, 1640-1646 and 1756-1762 repeat the same eleven keyword: value pairs. anthropic/ask (1388-1390) already demonstrates the rest-args forwarding style working in this module.

**Judge.** Verified: the eleven-option keyword list is repeated identically in four signatures plus three forwarding blocks, so one new API option means seven lock-step edits, and a keyword omitted from a forwarding block silently falls back to the default with no error (confirmed in csi: CHICKEN ignores unknown/missing keywords silently, so the explicit signatures give no typo protection today either). The proposed rest-args forwarding is behavior-safe -- extra keywords like betas: passed through to anthropic-request/body are ignored -- and the module itself already uses this exact pattern at anthropic/ask (1388-1390). Collapsing the option surface to its one real definition site (anthropic-request/body) removes a genuine silent-failure maintenance hazard; the only cost is that entry-point signatures no longer enumerate options, which the single definition site answers. Clear net maintainability win. Minor care needed: anthropic/stream must place stream: #t ahead of forwarded options, and anthropic/converse must extract tools before forwarding.

#### [refactoring/minor] `aux.base.scm:237` — letassoc/cdr syntax-rules pattern head says letassoc

The pattern of letassoc/cdr reads ((letassoc (searchexpr lstexpr) (else body ...)) ...). syntax-rules ignores the pattern head so behavior is unaffected, but the copy-paste slip makes the definition read as if it redefined letassoc, and grepping for letassoc call sites turns up this false hit. Change the pattern head to letassoc/cdr (or the conventional _).

**Evidence.** Code-level: R7RS/CHICKEN syntax-rules discards the first element of each rule pattern, so the mismatched head is inert; the fix is a one-word rename with zero behavioral risk (module compiles identically).

**Judge.** Confirmed at aux.base.scm:237: the letassoc/cdr rule's pattern head reads letassoc, a copy-paste slip from the letassoc macro just above. syntax-rules discards the pattern head so behavior is identical, but grepping for letassoc call sites does surface this false hit (verified), and the definition misleadingly reads as a redefinition of letassoc. A one-word rename to letassoc/cdr (or _) is zero-risk and a small but genuine clarity/greppability win — cheap enough to be worth doing.

#### [refactoring/minor] `aux.base.scm:415` — absent?: merge the two near-identical tree walkers

atom-absent? and struct-absent? duplicate the entire pair/vector/record traversal verbatim (including the 8-line fx-indexed vector loop); they differ only in where the (equal? v obj) test sits — at every node for compound v, only at leaves for atomic v. Compute the compound? flag once (it already is, at the dispatch on line 439) and use a single walker: (define (W obj) (cond ((and compound? (equal? v obj)) #f) ((pair? obj) (and (W (car obj)) (W (cdr obj)))) ((vector? obj) <one copy of the loop over W>) ((record-instance? obj) (W (record->vector obj))) (else (or compound? (not (equal? v obj)))))). Behavior is identical (for compound v the node test already covered the leaf; for atomic v the node test is skipped and the leaf test kept) and the function shrinks by ~12 lines with one traversal skeleton to maintain instead of two.

**Evidence.** Lines 416-426 and 427-438 of aux.base.scm are textually identical except for the ((equal? v obj) #f) head clause and the leaf expression ((not (equal? v obj)) vs #t); the surrounding dispatch (line 439) already distinguishes the two cases with a single boolean condition.

**Judge.** Verified: lines 416-438 of aux.base.scm duplicate the entire pair/vector/record traversal verbatim (including the 8-line fx-indexed vector loop), differing only in the head (equal? v obj) clause and the leaf expression. I ran the proposed merged walker against the compiled absent? on 20,000 randomized needle/haystack pairs plus edge cases (v='(), compound-node hits, vectors, nested structures): zero mismatches — the (and compound? (equal? v obj)) head clause plus (or compound? (not (equal? v obj))) leaf clause is exactly equivalent, and the dispatch at line 439 already computes the compound? boolean once. absent? backs the microkanren absento constraint, so keeping one traversal skeleton instead of two prevents future divergence when the traversal is extended; the cost is a single boolean-variable test per node, negligible next to the type dispatch and equal? already there. A clear ~12-line simplification with verified identical behavior.

#### [refactoring/minor] `aux.category.difflist.scm:14` — difflist record printer uses map for side effects

(map (μ v (D " ") (D v)) lst) is called purely for the display side effects and its result list is discarded; map's application order is unspecified by the standard, so the printed element order is only accidentally correct, and a throwaway list is allocated per print. Use (for-each (μ v (D " ") (D v)) lst), which guarantees left-to-right order and allocates nothing.

**Evidence.** Code-level: R7RS/CHICKEN specify for-each applies left to right while map's order of application is unspecified; the printer at aux.category.difflist.scm:9-15 relies on left-to-right to render ⸨ 1 2 3 ⸩ correctly.

**Judge.** Confirmed at aux.category.difflist.scm:14: map's result list is discarded and the printed order relies on map's unspecified application order, while for-each guarantees left-to-right and allocates no result list. It is a one-token change that turns accidentally-correct code into specified-correct idiomatic code; small, but pure win with zero downside.

#### [refactoring/minor] `aux.category.monad.scm:67` — <$> re-derives map/monad through <*>, costing an extra bind

(<$> f m) is defined as (<*> (M:return f) m), which expands to two binds (one to extract f from the freshly wrapped return, one over m), while map/monad three lines above is the identical operation with the identical argument order done in one bind. Replace with (define <$> map/monad): removes the duplication and one >>=/return round-trip per call, which for instances with non-trivial bind (prob consolidates on every bind, difflist materializes the list on every bind) is a real saving, and for prob avoids an extra consolidate pass.

**Evidence.** Code-level: (<*> (return f) m) = (do/monad (← g (return f)) (← x m) ,(g x)) — the first ← is a left-identity no-op that still executes a full >>= in instances like prob (which runs consolidate inside every >>=) and difflist (which runs (f '()) + map + foldr closure build inside every >>=). map/monad = (do/monad (← x mx) ,(f x)) is the same function computed with one bind.

**Judge.** Verified code-level and behaviorally: (<*> (M:return f) m) expands to two binds where the first is a left-identity no-op, while map/monad (defined 19 lines earlier in the same functor, identical argument order) is the same function in one bind; checked equal? results on the prob and difflist instances. (define <$> map/monad) is a strict improvement: it deletes a duplicated definition, states the fmap-from-bind derivation directly, and skips a full extra >>= per call, which for prob means one whole consolidate pass saved per <$>.

#### [refactoring/minor] `aux.category.monad.scm:79` — list->monad is monoids->monad instantiated at ('() , cons)

list->monad recurses with (do/monad (← xs* (list->monad xs)) ,(cons x xs*)) and base (M:return '()) — exactly the R loop of monoids->monad with mempty = '() and mappend = cons. Replace the whole definition with (define list->monad (monoids->monad '() cons)), deleting the duplicated recursion. Behavior is identical clause by clause.

**Evidence.** Code-level: monoids->monad's R is (match/first lst (() (M:return mempty)) ((,x . ,xs) (do/monad (← x* (R xs)) ,(mappend x x*)))); substituting mempty='() and mappend=cons yields list->monad's body verbatim (modulo the bound variable name xs*/x*).

**Judge.** The equivalence is exact — monoids->monad's R with mempty='() and mappend=cons is list->monad's body verbatim, and (monoids->monad '() cons) gives equal? results on empty and non-empty lists in the prob instance. Replacing 6 lines of duplicated recursion with a one-liner that names the relationship is a clarity win. One implementation caveat the suggestion omits: list->monad currently precedes monoids->monad in the file, so the definition must be moved after it (or eta-expanded to (define (list->monad lst) ((monoids->monad '() cons) lst))) since the functor body evaluates sequentially — trivial, but the implementer should know.

#### [refactoring/minor] `aux.cml.scm:935` — default-exn-handler's initial lambda duplicates uncaught-default-handler

The core's default-exn-handler parameter is initialized (lines 935-939) with a lambda whose body — print-error-message to (current-error-port) with the message (string-append "cml: thread " (tid->string ...) " died of an uncaught exception") — is character-for-character the body of TraceCML's exported uncaught-default-handler (lines 3546-3548), differing only in taking the tid as a parameter versus reading %cur-tid. The two copies must stay in sync (uncaught-default-handler is documented as "the message of the core's default-exn-handler") and can drift. Since uncaught-default-handler has no dependencies beyond tid->string, move its definition up to the threads section and initialize the parameter with (make-parameter (λ (e) (uncaught-default-handler %cur-tid e))), deleting the duplicated body.

**Evidence.** Lines 936-939: (make-parameter (λ (e) (print-error-message e (current-error-port) (string-append "cml: thread " (tid->string %cur-tid) " died of an uncaught exception"))));
lines 3546-3548: (define (uncaught-default-handler tid e) (print-error-message e (current-error-port) (string-append "cml: thread " (tid->string tid) " died of an uncaught exception"))). Identical bodies modulo the tid source; the comment at 3545 itself says it is "the message of the core's default-exn-handler".

**Judge.** Verified: the parameter initializer at lines 936-939 and uncaught-default-handler at 3546-3548 are character-for-character identical modulo the tid source, and the comment at 3545 explicitly documents that the two must mirror each other ('the message of the core's default-exn-handler') — a real drift hazard. The fix is trivially safe: the file is one module exporting * so no export list changes, tid->string is defined at line 258 (before 935), both procedures import and run correctly from the compiled module, and default-exn-handler only fires after load so (make-parameter (λ (e) (uncaught-default-handler %cur-tid e))) is semantically identical. Small, zero-risk deduplication that collapses a documented must-stay-in-sync coupling into one definition; clear net win.

#### [refactoring/minor] `aux.continuation.delimited.scm:53` — delimcc-foldr syntax-rules pattern head says delimcc-fold

The macro defined as delimcc-foldr (line 51) has its syntax-rules pattern spelled (delimcc-fold bexpr ...) at line 53. The head position is ignored by syntax-rules so behavior is unaffected, but the mismatched name is a copy-paste残 that misleads readers grepping for delimcc-fold (the same slip exists in the commented delimcc-foldr§ in aux.stream.scm:23). Rename the pattern head to delimcc-foldr, matching the fix already suggested for letassoc/cdr in round 1.

**Evidence.** aux.continuation.delimited.scm lines 51-53: (define-syntax delimcc-foldr (syntax-rules () ((delimcc-fold bexpr ((each acc) fbody ...) body ...) ...))). Verified the macro itself works: (delimcc-foldr 0 ((e a) (+ e a)) (yield/extract 1) (yield/extract 2) (yield/extract 3)) => 6.

**Judge.** Verified: aux.continuation.delimited.scm line 51 defines delimcc-foldr but the pattern at line 53 reads (delimcc-fold bexpr ...). syntax-rules ignores the head position so behavior is correct (and the macro works when exercised), making this cosmetic — but it is a genuine copy-paste slip, the fix is a single-token rename with zero behavioral risk, and a pattern head that names a nonexistent macro actively misleads anyone reading or grepping the macro definitions. Trivial cost, small but real clarity gain; worth folding into the next touch of the file.

#### [refactoring/minor] `aux.hansei.scm:49` — Delete the two stale commented-out probcc-next-value versions

Lines 49-69 hold two whole `#;`-datum-commented implementations of probcc-next-value in the middle of the module. The first even references `λ1/non-overlapping` and `match/non-overlapping` shapes that do not exist under those names in aux.base (the real name is λ1-match/non-overlapping), so neither version would compile if uncommented. Twenty-one lines of dead, unbuildable code between probcc-explore and probcc-normalize should be deleted (git history keeps them).

**Evidence.** aux.hansei.scm:54 uses (λ1/non-overlapping ...); grep of aux.base.scm shows only λ1-match/non-overlapping and match/non-overlapping — `λ1/non-overlapping` is bound nowhere in the repository, so the commented code is not a working alternative, just clutter.

**Judge.** Verified: aux.hansei.scm lines 49-69 hold two #;-commented implementations, and grep confirms λ1/non-overlapping (used at line 54) is bound nowhere in the repository — the real macro is λ1-match/non-overlapping in aux.base.scm:79 — so the first version would not even compile if uncommented. Twenty-one lines of dead, unbuildable code sitting between two live definitions is pure clutter; git history preserves them. Minor but a clear clarity win with zero risk.

#### [refactoring/minor] `aux.kanren.date.scm:542` — Hoist WEEK numeral and weekday-anchor helper beside DAY

The module hoists (build-num 86400) into the constant DAY (line 391) precisely so the seam never rebuilds it, but the weekday layer rebuilds its own constants inside project° bodies on every goal run: (number->numeral days/week) appears three times (day-of-week° line 544, day-of-week/within° lines 585 and 592), and the anchor expression (number->numeral (vector-ref weekday-epoch-day (weekday-index w*))) is duplicated between day-of-week° (line 542) and day-of-week/within° (line 590). Define (define WEEK (build-num 7)) next to DAY and a layer-0 helper (define (weekday-anchor w) (number->numeral (vector-ref weekday-epoch-day (weekday-index w)))), then use them at the four sites. Removes the duplication that would let the two relations' anchors drift apart, and matches the module's own DAY precedent.

**Evidence.** Lines 541-544: (stride° (number->numeral (vector-ref weekday-epoch-day (weekday-index w*))) (number->numeral days/week) day); lines 589-593 repeat the same anchor and step for stride/≤°; line 583-587 rebuilds (number->numeral days/week) again in the L-ground branch. All sit inside project° bodies, i.e. are re-evaluated at every goal invocation, while the identical-role constant DAY (line 391) is hoisted.

**Judge.** All cited facts check out in the source: (number->numeral days/week) is rebuilt at lines 543, 585 and 591, and the nontrivial anchor expression (number->numeral (vector-ref weekday-epoch-day (weekday-index w*))) is duplicated verbatim between day-of-week° (line 542) and day-of-week/within° (line 590), all inside project° bodies re-evaluated on every goal run. No WEEK constant exists, the layer-0 helpers it needs are defined earlier in the file, and DAY (line 391) is the module's own explicit precedent for hoisting exactly this kind of constant. The performance gain is negligible (building a numeral for 7 is cheap), but as the minor refactoring it is labeled as, it removes a real drift risk between the two relations' anchor expressions and follows established module style — a modest but genuine clarity win at essentially zero risk.

#### [refactoring/minor] `aux.kanren.micro.scm:429` — μkanren-ext-T is an observably identical re-implementation of μkanren-ext-T+

μkanren-ext-T (lines 429-435, called only from μkanren-absento+ at line 439) and μkanren-ext-T+ (lines 330-334) compute the same function: scan T, return '() if an entry (α* . tag*) with (equal? (μkanren-state-find α* s) α) and (μkanren-tag-equal? tag tag*) exists, else ((α . tag)). ext-T's extra clauses change nothing observable: its clause 2 with same var but different tag just continues the scan (exactly what ext-T+'s fall-through clause does, since ext-T+'s clause-2 guard requires tag equality too), and its clause 3 (same tag, different var) merely switches to ext-T+ for the rest of the list, which performs the same scan. Every path terminates with the same '() or ((α . tag)) result. Delete μkanren-ext-T and call μkanren-ext-T+ from μkanren-absento+ — one less near-duplicate of subtle constraint-store code to keep in sync (the file already has four ext-*/verify-* scan variants: ext-A, ext-T, ext-T+, ext-D).

**Evidence.** Case analysis over match/first clauses: ext-T clause 2 (find(α*)=α): tag-equal → '() [= ext-T+ clause 2]; different tag → recurse rest [= ext-T+ clause 3, since its clause-2 guard (and (equal? ...) (tag-equal ...)) fails]. ext-T clause 3 (tag-equal, find(α*)≠α) → (μkanren-ext-T+ α tag T* s), the same continuing scan. Clause 4 and the empty case are literally ext-T+'s clauses 3 and 1. All absento behaviors (dedup across repeated absent° calls, propagation through pairs/vectors/records, subsumption into D for typed vars) were exercised in the probe runs and the existing test suite and are decided solely by the shared '()/((α . tag)) result.

**Judge.** Verified by clause-by-clause analysis of the source: both functions are pure scans over T that return '() exactly when an entry satisfies (equal? (μkanren-state-find α* s) α) and (μkanren-tag-equal? tag tag*), and ((α . tag)) otherwise. ext-T's diff-tag clause 2 continues the scan just as ext-T+'s fall-through does (ext-T+'s clause-2 guard requires tag equality too), its clause 3 hands the rest to ext-T+ which performs the same scan, and its remaining clauses are literally ext-T+'s. The sole caller, μkanren-absento+ (line 439), depends only on that shared '()/((α . tag)) result. Deleting the 7-line near-duplicate of subtle constraint-store code and calling μkanren-ext-T+ is a small but clear net win: one fewer scan variant to keep in sync among the four ext-*/verify-* variants already in the file, with zero behavior change.

#### [refactoring/minor] `aux.kanren.micro.show.scm:132` — Tabular layout misfires on a single list-shaped answer

μkanren-answers-tabular? (lines 132-136) declares the answer set "a relation deserving a real table" whenever every answer is a ground list of the same length > 1 — which is vacuously true for ANY single ground list answer. Since μkanren-answers-layout (lines 146-150) checks 'tuples before the width-based blocks/table choice, a lone answer like (1 2) or a 36-element list is rendered as a fake relation with fabricated headers c0, c1, ... c35 (one column per list element) instead of as one term — for the wide case this is exactly the outcome the comment above μkanren-answers-layout argues against. Concrete change: in μkanren-answers-tabular? require either explicit headers or at least two answers, e.g. add (pair? (cdr answers)) to the conjunction and pass headers down so μkanren-answers->markdown/table's tabular branch fires for a single answer only when the caller supplied headers (as μkanren-run/markdown/table does).

**Evidence.** (display (μkanren-run/markdown (q 1) (=° q (list 1 2)))) renders the answer table as "| # | c0 | c1 |" / "| 1 | `1` | `2` |", and a single 36-element list answer renders as a 36-column table with headers c0..c35, while the mermaid views correctly show one term. With 4 answers of 2-tuples the tabular layout is right and useful.

**Judge.** Reproduced: a single answer (1 2) renders as a fake relation '| # | c0 | c1 |', and a lone wide list would get one fabricated column per element — exactly the unreadable-table outcome the module's own layout comment argues against, and inconsistent with the mermaid views that correctly show one term. The tabular heuristic's stated rationale ('the shape a query over a database of facts produces') genuinely requires plural answers or caller-supplied headers, so requiring (pair? (cdr answers)) or explicit headers is a small, well-targeted fix, not churn. Minor, but a clear output-quality win.

#### [refactoring/minor] `aux.machine.secd.scm:185` — Dump-popping clause duplicated in all three SECD transitions

The `(null? C)`-with-non-void-dump clause is repeated verbatim three times: →/interpreted lines 185-190, →/compiled lines 357-362, →/compiled⁺ lines 449-454 — six lines each building `extend-dump` inline via dbind/status and pushing `(car S)` onto the dump's stack. Extract one module-level helper, e.g. `(define pop-dump (λ (S D) ((dbind/status (λ (_ S₁ E₁ C₁ D₁) (make-status (cons (car S) S₁) E₁ C₁ D₁))) D)))`, and replace each clause body with `(pop-dump S D)`. Removes ~15 lines and makes the shared dump discipline of the three machines explicit; behavior is identical (pure record construction, no laziness involved).

**Evidence.** Code-level: the three clauses are textually identical (same dbind/status receiver, same make-status), differing only in the lexical S and D they close over, which the helper takes as arguments.

**Judge.** Verified: the (null? C) clause bodies at aux.machine.secd.scm:185-190, 357-362 and 449-454 are textually identical six-line blocks building the same dbind/status receiver and make-status, differing only in the lexical S and D. The proposed pop-dump helper taking S and D is a pure record construction with no laziness, so behavior is exactly preserved, and the module already hoists shared machinery (dbind/status, dbind/closure, status-printer) to module level, so a module-level helper fits the existing style. It names the one shared rule of all three machines (restore the caller's status and push the return value) and removes about a dozen lines. Minor but a real clarity win, not churn.

#### [refactoring/minor] `aux.machine.secd.scm:389` — compile⁺ duplicates five of compile's six clauses verbatim

compile (line 318) and compile⁺ (line 389) are identical except for the %Comb₊ clause: compile⁺ adds the Enter/Exit and Position&Apply special cases, and its else branch is exactly compile's generic `(,@(C rand) ,@(C rator) ,(Apply))`. The %Id₋, %Id₊, %Lambda₊, %If₊, %J₊ and error clauses are duplicated verbatim. Extract a parameterised compiler, e.g. (define (compiler comb) (rec C (λ (e) (cond ... ((%Comb₊? e) (comb C e)) ...)))) and define compile = (compiler generic-comb) and compile⁺ = (compiler optimising-comb), where optimising-comb falls back to generic-comb in its else branch. This mirrors the already-reported duplication between →/compiled and →/compiled⁺ and removes the same drift risk from the compilers.

**Evidence.** Side-by-side of lines 318-327 and 389-402: the only differing clause is %Comb₊ (line 324 vs 395-399); compile⁺'s else sub-branch at line 399 is exactly compile's line 324. No behavior change involved; the 13 secd suite tests exercise both compilers and would guard the extraction.

**Judge.** Verified by side-by-side read of aux.machine.secd.scm lines 318-327 and 389-402: only the %Comb₊ clause differs, and compile⁺'s else sub-branch (line 399) is exactly compile's generic Comb clause (line 324), so the generic combination compilation lives in two places. The proposed extraction has a single, natural variation point (how a combination is compiled, with the optimising version falling back to the generic one), which directly expresses the module's own framing that compile⁺ is 'the optimising compile' — a mild but real clarity win rather than added indirection — and the 13 secd suite tests guard the behavior-preserving change. Minor, but a clear net positive.

#### [refactoring/minor] `aux.nondeterministic.scm:19` — describe's (left ...) statistic is always 0

The `describe` thunk reports `(left ,(length pool))`, but its only call site is `((null? pool) (cc (describe)))` inside `fail` (line 28), so `pool` is guaranteed empty whenever describe runs and the `left` entry is always 0. Either drop the field or, if the intent was to expose remaining alternatives, describe would have to be callable elsewhere. As it stands it is dead output that suggests information it can never carry.

**Evidence.** Code-level: grep shows `describe` invoked exactly once, guarded by `(null? pool)`. Empirically, every stats block printed across 8 varied letnondeterministic runs (DFS, BFS, cut, promise, continue?, empty choices) shows `(left 0)`.

**Judge.** Correct: `describe` has exactly one call site, `((null? pool) (cc (describe)))` at aux.nondeterministic.scm:28, so `(length pool)` is guaranteed 0 whenever it runs — the field is dead output that misleadingly suggests a count of remaining alternatives. Nothing depends on it: no test asserts on the stats block (letnondeterministic.scm never mentions tried/accepted/left; `(left 0)` shows up only in a regenerated HTML test-output page). Dropping the field is a one-line, zero-risk clarity fix that removes a lie from the reported statistics; minor, but a net win rather than churn.

#### [refactoring/minor] `aux.stream.series.scm:64` — series:∫ and series:∂ hand-roll stream:zip-with

series:∫ (lines 64-72) and series:∂ (lines 74-82) each define a private two-stream walker (I / D) via nested stream:dest/car+cdr that is exactly stream:zip-with specialized to / and *: series:∫ = (λ§ (s) (stream:cons 0 ((stream:zip-with /) s (series:from 1)))) and series:∂ = (λ§ (s) ((stream:zip-with *) (stream:cdr s) (series:from 1))). The only behavioral difference is that the hand-rolled walkers stop cleanly at the end of a finite stream, which stream:zip-with today does not (that is the round-1 finding 'stream:zip-with applies op to '() past finite streams'). Once that fix lands, both definitions collapse to the one-liners above, removing ~16 lines of duplicated destructuring; do them together so finite-polynomial integration/differentiation keeps terminating.

**Evidence.** Code-level: series:∫'s I emits (/ scar ncar) pairwise against (series:from 1) prefixed by 0; series:∂'s D emits (* scar ncar) of (stream:cdr s) against (series:from 1) — precisely stream:zip-with's element rule (aux.stream.sicp.scm lines 170-176). Correctness of the zip formulation is pinned by the existing trig/exponential suite (taylor/sine = series:∫ taylor/cosine, tested in test/stream.sicp.scm lines 323-340).

**Judge.** Verified: the proposed one-liners produce output equal? to the stock series:∫/series:∂ (aux.stream.series.scm:64-82) over 20 terms of taylor/exponential and taylor/sine, and they correctly support the mutually recursive taylor/sine//taylor/cosine define-delay (the head 0 is emitted without forcing the argument stream, so laziness is preserved). The stated coupling is real and load-bearing: stream:zip-with (aux.stream.sicp.scm:170-176) currently calls stream:car past the end of a finite stream and errors, while the hand-rolled walkers stop cleanly (∫ of finite (1 2 3) → (0 1 1 1), ∂ → (2 6)); so this must land together with the round-1 zip-with finite-stream fix, exactly as the suggestion says. Given that, replacing ~16 lines of nested stream:dest/car+cdr with the same combinator the module already uses for series:+, series:- and series:* is a genuine clarity win, not churn.

#### [refactoring/minor] `aux.stream.series.scm:219` — Pythagorean-triples binds slow-enumeration it never uses; stale commented duplicate at line 324

Pythagorean-triples (lines 212-226) binds slow-enumeration to stream:enumerate-upper but only ever uses fast-enumeration, so the binding (and the dangling close-paren formatting it forces at line 225) can be deleted, leaving the let with just F, nats and the weighted enumeration. In the same file, line 324 is a commented-out duplicate of the numbers/fibs/∞ definition directly above it (the pre-:⁺ spelling) and can be deleted, matching the cleanup already applied elsewhere in the library.

**Evidence.** grep shows slow-enumeration appears only at its binding site (line 219) and nowhere else in the repository; line 324 is byte-for-byte the expansion of the live definition at lines 322-323.

**Judge.** Verified: grep over src/ shows slow-enumeration occurs only at its binding site (aux.stream.series.scm:219), so the let binding is dead code that misleads readers into looking for a use, and line 324 is a commented-out pre-:⁺ duplicate of the live numbers/fibs/∞ definition directly above (lines 322-323). Both deletions are zero-risk (stream:enumerate-upper itself remains exported from (aux stream sicp) for anyone who wants the slow variant) and match cleanup style used elsewhere. Minor but genuine clarity win, not churn.

## Refuted claims

- `aux.kanren.micro.scm` — Unification conflates vectors with same-shaped record instances (refuted): The behavior reproduces exactly as claimed — I ran a script in the scratchpad against the compiled scratch repo: (=° (make-point 1 2) (vector 'point 1 2)) succeeds with ((λ (α) α)), and a fresh var can be bound to the record then "confirmed" against the vector, while (equal? (make-point 1 2) #(point 1 2)) is #f. BUT it is intended, tested, source-commented behavior, not a bug: (1) src/aux.kanren.m

## Rejected suggestions

- `aux.anthropic.scm` — JSON escape: emit unescaped runs as substrings: Correct technique and the 2.6-4.4x speedup is plausible, but the absolute gain is ~3ms per 200KB of payload on a path that immediately precedes a network round trip measured in seconds -- negligible in practice. Unlike the utf8-length change it costs clarity rather than gaining it: the transparent per-char dispatch becomes a scan-for-next-escape loop wrapped around the same dispatch. Churn for milliseconds; skip.

- `aux.kanren.date.scm` — day+second° ground branch pays a relational <° it already knows: judge did not address it

- `aux.stream.sicp.scm` — Four duplicated enumeration skeletons in enumerate-* : judge did not address it

- `chicken-simdjson.h` — Embed the value union in the DOM node: ~17% faster parse: judge did not address it

- `chicken-timsort.c` — C_timsort and C_timsort_vector duplicate their entire setup prologue: judge did not address it

- `aux.tabling.scm` — tabled/get-hidden-hash-table sentinel is dead code: The premise is factually wrong: the suggestion's grep missed real uses. aux.machine.env.scm dispatches on the sentinel in four places (E₀, E⁺, E->alist, and extend's E₁, which deliberately answers it by MERGING its own table with the wrapped environment's), and test/tabling.scm exercises the same convention at lines 144, 157, 166, 168. The comment at line 43 ('a tabled procedure MAY answer it... see λH below') accurately describes this opt-in, per-procedure protocol: the body checks for the sentinel and answers with (λH). Deleting the binding breaks the build; hardwiring the dispatch into define-tabled would break E₁'s merge semantics. Nothing to fix.

- `aux.kanren.micro.scm` — Note: tabling/memoization and core verified correct (no further bugs found): Not an actionable suggestion — it proposes no code change and says so itself ('No action needed; this entry is informational'). Its verification content is legitimate (the probes it cites match what I observed of the same modules) and it has value only as carried context for a later review round, not as an item on a fix list, so it should be judged out rather than acted on.

- `aux.cml.scm` — Four copies of the hand-message-to-blocked-receiver switch sequence: judge did not address it

- `aux.cml.scm` — Three copies of the set-unchosen-nack-cvars loop: judge did not address it

- `aux.unittest.scm` — ⊨ rejects truthy non-#t values, forcing (and ... #t) boilerplate: judge did not address it

- `aux.anthropic.scm` — Every decode path validates the JSON twice: parse re-runs well-formed?: The redundancy is real and the benchmark reproduces (validation ~52% of parse time), but the absolute win is negligible: ~4ms per 80KB response against a multi-second API round trip, and microseconds per SSE frame since frames are small. Parse is never on a hot loop outside per-response decode. Against that, the module comment (lines 62-66) documents the gate as load bearing: chicken-simdjson.cpp has no try/catch, so an unguarded parse of malformed input aborts the process with an uncatchable SIGABRT. Making anthropic-json/parse unconditionally self-defending is a deliberate single choke point; an ungated internal parse* reintroduces exactly the process-killing footgun the design eliminates, in exchange for a saving invisible next to network latency. Wrong trade for this module.

- `aux.category.ziplist.scm` — ziplist <*> is O(n^3): length/>? walk per index, inner bind per outer element: judge did not address it

- `aux.category.writer.scm` — Writer >>= matches records reflectively; accepts any 2-field record: judge did not address it

- `chicken-simdjson.cpp` — count_elements pre-scan makes DOM building O(size x depth): judge did not address it

- `aux.timsort.scm` — timsort/vector/key/gen duplicates the %timsort-keys extraction loop: judge did not address it

- `aux.sxml.scm` — sxml-tree's title argument is computed but never emitted: judge did not address it

- `aux.kanren.date.scm` — Civil round-trip validation duplicated at four sites: judge did not address it

- `aux.tabling.scm` — Memo hit path does two hash lookups; sentinel makes it one: judge did not address it

- `aux.kanren.micro.show.scm` — Mermaid emitters duplicate the node/edge builder machinery: judge did not address it

- `aux.games.dice-of-doom.scm` — game-tree's closure-private memo table grows unboundedly and cannot be cleared: The observation is factually correct: the table at aux.games.dice-of-doom.scm:93 is closure-private, hash-table-ref/store only adds entries, and no exported binding can clear it. But it is not worth acting on. The construction is deliberate and documented in the module header (lines 20-21): the circular players list cannot be an equal? hash key, so game-tree keys on players-rotation via a private hash-table-ref/store table instead of define-tabled. Process-lifetime memoization is also the faithful Land of Lisp design this module ports — cross-call sharing is what makes repeated searches of the same positions cheap, including across games on the same board — and the rest of the codebase has the identical property everywhere (define-tabled's λH escape hatch is body-scoped and never exported; neighbors and the tabled environments in (aux machine env) all retain forever with no reset convention). Adding a game-tree/reset! export or per-game closures would invent new public API for a hypothetical long-running-server use of a pedagogical game demo, breaking symmetry with every other memoized procedure in the library while fixing no observed problem — the suggestion itself concedes results are correct and calls it purely resource cleanup. This is churn.

- `aux.cml.scm` — Extract %wake-waiter! for the claim-enqueue-cleanup wake sequence: The premise is wrong: the three sites are not identical. %poll-io! (aux.cml.scm:1462-1464) runs the cleanup BEFORE %enqueue-thread!, while %barrier-complete! (1966-1968) and %chan-out-take! (3082-3084) enqueue first and run cleanup after — the suggestion's own evidence quotes show this. The proposed helper (enqueue, then cleanup) would silently swap the ordering in %poll-io!, and since a cleanup can enqueue other threads, that changes observable FIFO wake order at that site; the suggestion is even internally inconsistent, calling %poll-time! a variant 'which enqueues before running cleanup' when that is exactly what its helper does. Only two sites are truly identical, each three lines, and the vector-field extraction stays at the call sites anyway because the layouts differ, so the helper saves almost nothing while either changing behavior or needing two variants. Churn with a latent behavior change; not worth doing.

- `aux.category.monad.scm` — Expand (← var ,e) to let1 via left identity: 5.1x on list monad: The 5x speedup is real (verified: 2086-2105 ms vs 443-444 ms on the quoted benchmark), but the premise that left identity holds observationally for every instance is false: for (aux category prob), >>= consolidates duplicates and multiplies by the 1.0 in return, so (>>= (return 3) g) yields ((#t . 0.5) (#f . 0.5)) where the proposed let expansion yields ((#t . 1/2) (#f . 1/2)) (equal? => #f), and an unconsolidated tail ((a . 0.25) (a . 0.25) (b . 0.5)) stays unconsolidated instead of becoming ((a . 0.5) (b . 0.5)). do/monad is a functor over arbitrary instances, so baking left identity into the macro silently changes prob results (e.g. (do/monad (← x ,3) (coin/tf 1/2))) and any future normalizing/effectful instance. The library already provides the fast pure binding explicitly — the (let var expr) clause at aux.category.monad.scm:39 expands to exactly what is proposed and is what test/category-list.scm uses — so the win is available today by writing let, while ← ,e retains its meaning of routing through the instance's return/>>=.

- `aux.fds.unionfind.scm` — unionfind-↑!/↑ do 3 hash lookups per node; sentinel ref/default makes find 2.3x faster: judge did not address it

- `aux.judy.scm` — Judy walk threads a dead PValue handle through JF/JN/JL/JP: judge did not address it

- `aux.games.dice-of-doom.scm` — get-ratings/αβ-max and /αβ-min are hand-maintained mirror copies: The duplication is real (lines 302-322 are token-level mirrors modulo the comparison, the tightened limit, and which limit moves), but the proposed unification is a net clarity loss: it threads three higher-order parameters (cut?, tighten, and a values-swapping limits selector) plus call-with-values plumbing through one skeleton to reconstruct rate-position/αβ's fixed (upper lower) argument order. Two direct 10-line functions whose max/min symmetry is visible at a glance are easier to verify against the αβ algorithm than one generic skeleton with role-swapping lambdas. The module is moreover a deliberate port of Land of Lisp, which presents the max/min pair as separate definitions, and its header documents every deviation — this is finished book code, not an evolving αβ implementation, so the drift-risk argument carries little weight. The genuinely clearer unification would be negamax, which is a behavioral restructuring beyond this suggestion. As proposed: churn.

## Coverage notes

What each reviewer covered (including areas probed and found clean):

- [core r1] All four assigned modules read line-by-line and exercised with the custom csi (macro hygiene of let1/begin1/match-first/μ, Y, remove-duplicates, enumerate, mappair, pairwise-different?, commons helpers foldl1/map-with-index/remove-duplicates-last/symbol∼subscripts, aux.variable printing all verified OK). Not deeply exercised: the SXML/HTML emission path of unittest/✓ (depends on (aux sxml), outside my modules), define-macro/define-macro-ir under separate compilation (import-for-syntax interactions), and the documentation-framework setter path beyond a smoke read. Deliberately not reported as bugs (judged intended designs): ⊨ requiring exactly #t rather than truthiness, (μ () ...) expanding to a one-argument lambda, letport/string's fragile `out` literal, and match-case-simple* clauses appearing after an else clause.
